# 01 — Geometry Foundations  
## Part 1 / ?

# 1. Introduction

This file establishes the geometric backbone used throughout the Yang–Mills mass-gap program.  
It consolidates and expands the foundational material needed before curvature, Bakry–Émery calculus, functional inequalities, or lattice gauge structure can be formulated.

The goal is to define, rigorously and from first principles:

1. Finite-dimensional configuration manifolds  
2. Riemannian metrics  
3. Volume measures  
4. Gradients, Laplacians, Hessians  
5. Diffusion generators  
6. Dirichlet forms  
7. Ricci curvature and the Bochner identity  
8. Product manifolds and curvature inheritance  
9. General interface for Gibbs measures

There is **no lattice** and **no gauge theory** yet — only the geometric foundation that both scalar and gauge models sit on.

---

# 2. Finite-Dimensional Riemannian Manifolds

Let \(M\) be a smooth, connected, finite-dimensional manifold of dimension \(n\).

## 2.1 Riemannian Metric

A Riemannian metric is a smooth assignment
\[
g_x : T_x M \times T_x M \to \mathbb{R}
\]
of inner products on each tangent space.

We denote:

- \(\langle v, w \rangle_x := g_x(v,w)\)  
- \(|v|_x^2 := \langle v,v\rangle_x\)

The metric induces:

- A norm on tangent vectors  
- Lengths of curves  
- Geodesics  
- The Levi–Civita connection  
- The Laplace–Beltrami operator  
- A canonical volume form \(d\mathrm{vol}_g\)

We assume throughout that:

- \(M\) is **complete** in the geodesic sense  
- \(M\) has **no boundary** (everything extends if boundaries exist, but complicate notation)

---

## 2.2 Volume and Integration

The Riemannian volume form \(d\mathrm{vol}_g\) is the measure uniquely determined by the metric.  
In local coordinates \((x^1,\dots,x^n)\) with metric tensor \(g_{ij}(x)\),

\[
d\mathrm{vol}_g(x) = \sqrt{\det(g_{ij}(x))}\, dx^1\cdots dx^n.
\]

Integration of functions \(f : M\to\mathbb{R}\) is defined as usual.

---

# 3. Differential Operators

All analytic structures that appear later — carré du champ, Bakry–Émery curvature, etc. — rely on the following baseline operators.

## 3.1 Gradient

For \(f\in C^\infty(M)\), the gradient \(\nabla f\in \Gamma(TM)\) is defined by
\[
\langle \nabla f(x), v\rangle_x = df_x(v) \quad \forall v\in T_x M.
\]

## 3.2 Divergence and Laplace–Beltrami Operator

For a vector field \(X\), the divergence is denoted \(\mathrm{div}(X)\).  
Then the Laplace–Beltrami operator is
\[
\Delta_g f = \mathrm{div}(\nabla f).
\]

Most formulas require the coordinate expression:
\[
\Delta_g f
= \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}} \sum_{i,j=1}^n
\partial_i\!\left( \sqrt{\det g}\, g^{ij}\partial_j f\right).
\]

## 3.3 Hessian

With \(\nabla\) the Levi–Civita connection, the Hessian is the symmetric 2-tensor:
\[
\nabla^2 f(X,Y) := X(Yf) - (\nabla_X Y)f.
\]

In normal coordinates at a point, Christoffel symbols vanish and the Hessian reduces (at that point) to the ordinary matrix of second derivatives.

---

# 4. Ricci Curvature and the Riemann Tensor

## 4.1 Riemann Tensor

Let \(R\) be the Riemann curvature tensor defined via
\[
R(X,Y)Z = \nabla_X\nabla_Y Z - \nabla_Y\nabla_X Z - \nabla_{[X,Y]}Z.
\]

## 4.2 Ricci Tensor

The Ricci curvature is the trace of the Riemann tensor:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}(X,Y)
= \sum_{i=1}^n \langle R(e_i,X)Y, e_i\rangle.
\]

In normal coordinates at a given point, the Ricci tensor captures the second-order correction to geodesic sphere volumes.

Ricci curvature lower bounds control heat kernel behavior, concentration inequalities, and functional inequalities.  
Later, it appears combined with \(\nabla^2 S\) to form the Bakry–Émery tensor.

---

# 5. Product Manifolds and Curvature

The Yang–Mills configuration manifold is an enormous product:  
\[
M = G^{E(\Lambda)}
\]
where \(G\) is compact and \(E(\Lambda)\) is the set of edges.

We summarize the geometry of product manifolds.

## 5.1 Product Metric

If \((M_i, g_i)_{i=1}^k\) are Riemannian manifolds,  
\[
M := \prod_{i=1}^k M_i, \quad
g := \bigoplus_{i=1}^k g_i.
\]

Tangent vectors split:
\[
T_x M = \bigoplus_{i=1}^k T_{x_i}M_i.
\]

## 5.2 Ricci Curvature of Product

Ricci curvature is computed factorwise:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_M = \bigoplus_{i=1}^k \mathrm{Ric}_{M_i}.
\]

Hence, if each factor satisfies
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_{M_i} \ge \kappa_i g_i,
\]
then
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_M \ge \min_i \kappa_i \, g.
\]

This is the formal origin of the Haar-induced curvature lower bound on \(G^{E(\Lambda)}\).

---

# 6. Gibbs Measures on Riemannian Manifolds

Given a potential \(S : M\to\mathbb{R}\), one defines the Gibbs measure
\[
d\mu = Z^{-1} e^{-S} d\mathrm{vol}_g, \qquad
Z = \int_M e^{-S}d\mathrm{vol}_g.
\]

We assume:

- \(S \in C^2(M)\)  
- \(S\) bounded below  
- \(Z < \infty\)

These assumptions ensure that the diffusion generator associated with \(\mu\) is well-defined and symmetric.

---

# 7. Diffusion Generator \(L\)

The drift-diffusion operator is
\[
Lf = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S, \nabla f\rangle.
\]

This operator is symmetric in \(L^2(\mu)\):
\[
\int_M f Lg \, d\mu = \int_M g Lf \, d\mu
= -\int_M \langle \nabla f, \nabla g\rangle d\mu.
\]

The carré du champ operator is

\[
\Gamma(f,g) = \langle \nabla f, \nabla g\rangle,
\qquad
\Gamma(f)=|\nabla f|^2.
\]

This constitutes the analytic heart of all later curvature inequalities.

---

# 8. Dirichlet Forms and Self-Adjointness

Define the pre-Dirichlet form on \(C^\infty_c(M)\):
\[
\mathcal{E}(f,g) = \int_M \langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle \, d\mu.
\]

Under our assumptions:

- \(\mathcal{E}\) is closable  
- its closure is a Dirichlet form  
- associated to a unique nonnegative self-adjoint operator \(-L\)

Thus \(-L\) generates a Markov semigroup \(P_t = e^{tL}\) on \(L^2(\mu)\).

This apparatus supports functional inequalities, spectral gaps, LSI, and curvature arguments in Parts I–III.

---

# 9. Bochner Identity (Geometric Side Only)

The Bochner formula for \(f\in C^\infty(M)\) states:

\[
\frac{1}{2}\Delta_g |\nabla f|^2
= \|\nabla^2 f\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
+ \langle \nabla f, \nabla \Delta_g f\rangle
+ \mathrm{Ric}(\nabla f,\nabla f).
\]

This yields control of second derivatives via Ricci curvature and will later be strengthened into the full Bakry–Émery identity once the potential \(S\) is introduced.

---

# 10. Bakry–Émery Tensor (Geometric Foundation)

Given a Gibbs potential \(S\), the Bakry–Émery Ricci tensor is:

\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu := \mathrm{Ric}_g + \nabla^2 S.
\]

The core idea:

- \(\mathrm{Ric}_g\) comes from geometry  
- \(\nabla^2 S\) comes from convexity of the action  
- Their sum yields curvature-like control on the diffusion generator \(L\)

This is the key to the curvature-dimension condition \(CD(\rho,\infty)\), functional inequalities, and spectral gaps.

---

# END OF PART 1

(Part 2 will continue with extended Bochner–BE calculus foundations, product curvature scaling, and preparation for interacting systems.)

# 01 — Geometry Foundations  
## Part 2 / ?

# 11. Curvature-Dimension Framework (Geometric Layer)

Before introducing potentials or Gibbs measures, we clarify the geometric component of the curvature–dimension condition.

## 11.1 Classical Curvature Bounds

A lower Ricci curvature bound
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_g \ge \kappa g
\]
with \(\kappa>0\) implies:

- Heat kernel bounds  
- Volume comparison  
- Spectral gap for the Laplace–Beltrami operator (Lichnerowicz)  
- Functional inequalities such as Poincaré and log-Sobolev

These geometric facts are used indirectly, because in Yang–Mills the curvature bound will **not** be global but instead appear **locally** in a small ball around the trivial configuration.

## 11.2 Bakry–Émery Curvature

When Gibbs measures are introduced, Ricci curvature alone is insufficient. The appropriate curvature notion is:

\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu := \mathrm{Ric}_g + \nabla^2 S.
\]

This tensor governs the convexity of entropy along the diffusion semigroup generated by \(L\).

A uniform lower bound
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g
\]
is equivalent to the curvature–dimension condition \(CD(\rho,\infty)\).

This leads to a hierarchy:

- **BE curvature** ⇒  
  \( \Gamma_2(f) \ge \rho \Gamma(f) \)

- **CD(ρ,∞)** ⇒  
  Poincaré, LSI, spectral gap, hypercontractivity  

- **Local CD(ρ,∞)** ⇒  
  Local FI usable with Lyapunov drift to obtain global FI

All of these rely on the geometric-to-analytic translation encoded in the Bochner and Bakry–Émery identities.

---

# 12. Extended Bochner Identity (Pre-BE Form)

The classical Bochner identity already establishes:

\[
\frac12 \Delta_g |\nabla f|^2
= \|\nabla^2 f\|^2_{\mathrm{HS}}
+ \langle \nabla f, \nabla (\Delta_g f)\rangle
+ \mathrm{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f).
\]

This describes how gradient energy evolves under the pure Laplace–Beltrami component.

When potentials are added and the diffusion generator becomes  
\[
Lf = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S, \nabla f\rangle,
\]
we gain additional contributions from \(\nabla^2 S\). These will appear fully in the Bakry–Émery identity.

---

# 13. Product Manifolds in Detail

The Yang–Mills manifold is a huge product
\[
M_\Lambda = \prod_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)} G,
\]
where \(G\) is a compact Lie group.

We justify rigorously:

## 13.1 Tangent Splitting

For \(U = (U_\ell)_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)}\),

\[
T_U M_\Lambda = \bigoplus_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)} T_{U_\ell}G.
\]

This isomorphism is canonical and smooth.

## 13.2 Product Metric

Given the bi-invariant metric \(g_G\), the product metric is

\[
g_\Lambda(U)\big( (v_\ell), (w_\ell)\big)
= \sum_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)} g_G(U_\ell)(v_\ell,w_\ell).
\]

Norm:

\[
|(v_\ell)|_{g_\Lambda}^2 = \sum_\ell |v_\ell|_{g_G}^2.
\]

This metric is smooth, complete, and its Levi–Civita connection is the product of single-site connections.

## 13.3 Product Ricci Curvature

Since each factor satisfies
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_G \ge \kappa_G g_G,
\]
the product satisfies
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_{g_\Lambda} \ge \kappa_G g_\Lambda.
\]

This is absolutely critical later:  
**Haar measure alone contributes a positive curvature direction at every site.**

---

# 14. Exponential Coordinates and Volume Measure

Exponential coordinates allow us to describe the geometry near the identity in \(G\) and hence near the trivial configuration in \(M_\Lambda\).

## 14.1 Normal Coordinates on \(G\)

Let \(X \mapsto \exp_G(X)\) define a chart near \(0\in\mathfrak{g}\).  
Then local coordinates \(\theta \mapsto U = \exp_G(\theta)\) satisfy:

- \(\theta=0\) corresponds to \(U=e\)  
- \(\partial_i g_{jk}(0)=0\)  
- Riemannian volume has Taylor expansion involving the Ricci tensor

## 14.2 Haar Measure Jacobian

In normal coordinates,
\[
d\mathrm{vol}_{g_G}(U) = J_G(\theta)\, d\theta,
\]
and
\[
J_G(\theta)
= 1 - \frac16 \mathrm{Ric}_{ij}(0)\,\theta^i\theta^j + O(|\theta|^3).
\]

Define Haar potential:
\[
S_H(\theta) := -\log J_G(\theta)
= \frac{1}{6}\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}(0)\,\theta^i\theta^j + O(|\theta|^3).
\]

Thus **Haar measure is strictly log-concave near the identity**, and its Hessian is proportional to the Ricci tensor.

---

# 15. Hessian of the Haar Potential (Single Site)

The quadratic form associated to the Haar potential satisfies:

\[
\nabla^2 S_H(0)(X,X)
= \frac{1}{3} \mathrm{Ric}_G(X,X)
\ge c_H |X|_G^2
\quad (c_H = \kappa_G/3).
\]

This is the origin of the “Haar mass term”.

When lifted to the entire lattice:

\[
S_{H,\Lambda}(X) = \sum_{\ell} S_H(X_\ell),
\]
\[
\nabla^2 S_{H,\Lambda}(0)
= \bigoplus_{\ell} \nabla^2 S_H(0)
\ge c_H I.
\]

Thus Haar curvature provides a **uniform convexity term** independent of volume.

---

# 16. Self-Adjointness and Semigroup Theory

A rigorous mass-gap framework requires precise control of the analytic objects.

## 16.1 Dirichlet Form Closability

Given completeness of \(M\) and smooth positive density \(e^{-S}\), the pre-Dirichlet form

\[
\mathcal{E}(f,g) = \int_M \langle \nabla f,\nabla g \rangle d\mu
\]

is closable; its closure is a Dirichlet form.

## 16.2 Self-Adjoint Operator

There exists a unique nonnegative self-adjoint operator \(-L\) satisfying

\[
\mathcal{E}(f,g) = \langle f, -Lg \rangle_{L^2(\mu)}.
\]

Thus \(L\) has:

- A complete orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions (discrete spectrum under mild confining conditions)
- A well-defined spectral gap \(\lambda_1\ge 0\)

## 16.3 Semigroup

The Markov semigroup \(P_t = e^{tL}\) satisfies:

- \(P_t 1 = 1\)  
- \(P_t\) preserves positivity  
- \(\|P_t\|_{L^2(\mu)} \le 1\)  
- \(\partial_t P_t f = L P_t f\)

These analytic tools allow curvature bounds to be translated into spectral information.

---

# 17. Tensorization and Locality

Nearly everything in Yang–Mills depends on the fact that:

- The configuration manifold is a finite product  
- All local quantities (action, curvature, Hessian) involve finitely many factors  
- Constants do **not** degrade with volume

## 17.1 Tensorization of Gradient and Hessian

Gradients split factorwise, i.e.

\[
\nabla f(U) = (\nabla_\ell f(U))_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)}.
\]

Hessians also split due to product geometry.

## 17.2 Tensorization of FI and Curvature

If each factor satisfies a curvature bound or LSI, the product satisfies the same inequality with **the same constant**.

This is a deep fact and guarantees:

> Haar curvature on each site induces a uniform curvature bound on the entire lattice manifold.

---

# END OF PART 2

# 01 — Geometry Foundations  
## Part 3 / ?

# 18. Full Bakry–Émery Identity (Geometry + Potential)

We now assemble geometry and potential into the full Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity, which is the analytic cornerstone of the curvature–dimension condition.

Let
\[
L f = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S,\nabla f\rangle,
\quad
\Gamma(f,g) = \langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle,
\quad
\Gamma_2(f) = \frac12\big(L\Gamma(f) - 2\Gamma(f,Lf)\big).
\]

The identity is:

\[
\Gamma_2(f)
= \|\nabla^2 f\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
+ \mathrm{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f)
+ \nabla^2 S(\nabla f,\nabla f).
\]

Equivalently,
\[
\Gamma_2(f)
= \|\nabla^2 f\|^2 + \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(\nabla f,\nabla f),
\]
with the Bakry–Émery tensor
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu = \mathrm{Ric}_g + \nabla^2 S.
\]

This identity provides:

- Geometric convexity from \(\mathrm{Ric}_g\)
- Potential convexity from \(\nabla^2 S\)
- A unified curvature structure governing functional inequalities

---

# 19. Curvature–Dimension Condition \(CD(\rho,\infty)\)

A diffusion generator satisfies the curvature–dimension condition  
\[
CD(\rho,\infty)
\]
if
\[
\Gamma_2(f) \ge \rho \Gamma(f)
\quad \text{for all smooth } f.
\]

Using the BE identity:
\[
\Gamma_2(f)
= \|\nabla^2 f\|^2 + \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(\nabla f,\nabla f),
\]
so the condition is equivalent to:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g.
\]

When \(\rho>0\), this inequality encodes both positive curvature and strict convexity.

---

# 20. Consequences of \(CD(\rho,\infty)\)

Assuming \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) with \(\rho>0\), one obtains:

## 20.1 Spectral Gap (Poincaré)

\[
\mathrm{Var}_\mu(f) \le \frac{1}{\rho}\int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
\]

The optimal constant equals the spectral gap of \(-L\).

## 20.2 Logarithmic Sobolev Inequality

\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2) \le \frac{2}{\rho}\int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
\]

Equivalent to hypercontractivity of the semigroup.

## 20.3 Exponential Convergence of the Semigroup

\[
\|P_t f - \mu(f)\|_{L^2(\mu)}
\le e^{-\rho t} \|f - \mu(f)\|_{L^2(\mu)}.
\]

## 20.4 Strong Concentration of Measure

Concentration inequalities follow, including Gaussian-type bounds.

These facts are the analytic foundation for the mass-gap argument later: curvature gives spectral control.

---

# 21. Local Curvature vs Global Curvature

For most interacting systems, including Yang–Mills, **global** curvature bounds fail.  
However, **local** curvature bounds often hold in a controlled region.

This distinction leads to three levels:

1. **Global CD(ρ,∞)** — extremely strong, rarely true for interacting systems  
2. **Local CD(ρ_loc,∞)** — true in a region where convexity dominates (e.g., small-field region)  
3. **Horizontal CD(ρ_loc,∞)** — curvature bound only in selected directions (e.g., gauge-invariant directions)

For Yang–Mills:

- Haar curvature + Wilson Hessian give **positive horizontal curvature near the trivial configuration**
- The BE bound holds only in a ball around \(U^{(0)}\), not globally
- But this ball’s radius and curvature constant are **uniform in volume**

Local curvature will be combined with Lyapunov drift to obtain global inequalities.

---

# 22. Horizontal/Restricted Curvature Framework

In gauge theories, one never gains curvature in the vertical (gauge) directions.  
Instead, the fundamental mechanism is:

> Gauge-invariant observables have gradients entirely contained in the horizontal subspace.

Thus, the curvature–dimension condition only needs to hold on horizontals.

Formally:

Let \(H_x \subset T_x M\) be a smooth distribution (horizontal subspace).  
Let \(f\) satisfy \(\nabla f(x) \in H_x\) for all \(x\).  
Then it suffices that
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)\big|_{H_x} \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} g(x)\big|_{H_x}
\quad\text{for } x\in\Omega.
\]

This is exactly what occurs in lattice Yang–Mills:

- Vertical = gauge directions  
- Horizontal = physical directions orthogonal to gauge orbits  
- \(\nabla f\) is always horizontal for gauge-invariant \(f\)  
- Haar + Hessian combine to give **positive curvature on horizontals**

This is the geometric skeleton underlying the local curvature theorem in Part II.

---

# 23. Locality of Curvature in Lattice Systems

For product manifolds:

- Curvature is a **local sum** of contributions from each factor  
- Hessians of local potentials are sums of local second variations  
- All constants depend only on **geometry of a single site** and **finite number of neighbors**

Thus:

> Curvature bounds derived near the identity are **volume-independent**.

This is crucial for mass-gap arguments, where all constants must persist along thermodynamic or continuum limits.

---

# 24. Geometry of Gibbs Measures with Local Interactions

Most interacting models (scalar fields, spin systems, lattice gases, gauge theories) share these features:

1. **Finite-dimensional factor spaces**  
2. **Product metric structure**  
3. **Local potentials** affecting finitely many coordinates  
4. **A reference measure** with known curvature (e.g., Gaussian, Haar, product form)  
5. **Convexity only in small regions**

Local curvature is verified by:

- Expanding the potential to second order around minimal configurations  
- Checking that negative Hessian directions are bounded  
- Combining with positive curvature from reference measure  
- Ensuring continuity to extend the bound to a neighborhood

This entire methodology is independent of the existence of gauge structure or group structure; gauge theory simply adds the horizontal/vertical complication.

---

# 25. Continuity Lemmas for Curvature Bounds

To extend curvature bounds from a point to a neighborhood, one uses:

## 25.1 Continuity of Ricci and Hessians

On finite-dimensional manifolds:

- \(\mathrm{Ric}_g\) is smooth  
- \(\nabla^2 S\) is smooth  
- Their sum \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu\) is smooth

## 25.2 Perturbation of Eigenvalues

If a symmetric bilinear form \(A(x)\) depends smoothly on \(x\), then:

- Its minimal eigenvalue \(\lambda_{\min}(A(x))\) is continuous  
- A strict lower bound at one point extends to a neighborhood

These facts show:

> If \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U^{(0)})\big|_{H_{U^{(0)}}} \ge \rho_0 > 0\),  
> then there exists \(r>0\) such that  
> \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)\big|_{H_U} \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} > 0\) for all \(U\in B_r(U^{(0)})\).

This is exactly the logic used in the local curvature theorem.

---

# 26. Preparatory Lemmas for Interacting Systems

We record several lemmas repeatedly used later.

## 26.1 Tensorization Lemma

If each factor satisfies \(CD(\rho,\infty)\), then the product satisfies \(CD(\rho,\infty)\).

## 26.2 Local Curvature Patch Lemma

If there exists:

- A point \(x_0\in M\)  
- A subspace distribution \(H_x\)  
- A curvature bound \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x_0)\big|_{H_{x_0}} \ge \rho_0\)

then by continuity there exists a ball \(B_r(x_0)\) such that the same bound holds with \(\rho_{\mathrm{loc}}=\rho_0/2\).

## 26.3 Local Log-Concavity of Measure

If the potential \(S\) satisfies \(\nabla^2 S(x) \ge -C I\), and the reference measure is log-concave near \(x_0\), then the total curvature retains concavity near \(x_0\) provided \(C\) is small relative to curvature of reference.

This describes the Haar + Wilson convexity competition.

---

# 27. Summary of the Geometry Foundations

We now have all the tools that **Part II (lattice YM geometry)** and **Part III (global functional inequalities)** rely on:

1. Riemannian manifolds, volumes, gradients, Laplacians  
2. Gibbs measures and diffusion generators  
3. Carré du champ and \(\Gamma_2\)  
4. Bochner and BE identity  
5. Ricci + Hessian curvature structure  
6. Product manifolds and uniform curvature inheritance  
7. Horizontal curvature concept  
8. Continuity and local curvature propagation  
9. Locality of interactions in lattice models  

These foundations remain valid for all parts of the full Yang–Mills mass gap proof stack.

---

# 01 — Geometry Foundations  
## Part 4 / 4 (Final)

# 28. Quotient Geometry: Symmetries and Reduction

Many physical systems, particularly gauge theories, contain redundancies: different configurations represent the same physical state. Mathematically, these redundancies arise from the action of a Lie group \(G_{\mathrm{sym}}\) on the configuration manifold \(M\).

## 28.1 Group Action

Let a Lie group \(\mathcal{G}\) act smoothly on \((M,g)\):
\[
\mathcal{G} \times M \to M,\qquad (h,x)\mapsto h\cdot x.
\]

Assume:

- The action is **free** on an open dense subset (regular configurations),
- The action is **isometric**, i.e.
  \[
  g_{h\cdot x}\big(d(h\cdot)_x v,\, d(h\cdot)_x w\big) = g_x(v,w).
  \]

In lattice YM, \(\mathcal{G}\) is the lattice gauge group.

## 28.2 Vertical Subspace

The tangent to the orbit at \(x\) is the **vertical subspace**:
\[
V_x := \{ X^\sharp(x) : X\in \mathfrak{g}\},
\]
where \(X^\sharp\) is the fundamental vector field generated by \(X\).

Physically: infinitesimal gauge transformations.

## 28.3 Horizontal Subspace

Define the **horizontal subspace** as the orthogonal complement:
\[
H_x := V_x^\perp \subset T_x M.
\]

Important properties:

- \(T_x M = H_x \oplus V_x\) orthogonally
- Horizontal distributions transform equivariantly under the group
- Gradients of **invariant functions** lie entirely in \(H_x\)

This horizontal structure is the analytic stand-in for “physical degrees of freedom.”

---

# 29. Gauge-Invariant Functions and Horizontal Gradients

## 29.1 Invariant Functions

A function \(f : M\to\mathbb{R}\) is invariant if
\[
f(h\cdot x) = f(x)\quad \forall h\in\mathcal{G}.
\]

## 29.2 Derivative Vanishes on Vertical Directions

Differentiating the identity \(f(h(t)\cdot x) = f(x)\) at \(t=0\) for a one-parameter subgroup \(h(t) = \exp(tX)\):

\[
df_x(X^\sharp(x)) = 0.
\]

Thus all vertical components of \(\nabla f\) vanish.

## 29.3 Gradient is Horizontal

By definition of the gradient via the Riemannian metric:

\[
\langle \nabla f(x), v \rangle_x = df_x(v).
\]

Since \(df_x\) annihilates every vertical vector, \(\nabla f(x)\in H_x\).

This is the core mechanism that allows curvature estimates **restricted to \(H_x\)** to control all invariant observables.

---

# 30. Curvature Restricted to Horizontal Directions

Given the Bakry–Émery tensor:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu = \mathrm{Ric}_g + \nabla^2 S,
\]
only its restriction to horizontal vectors matters for invariant observables:

\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)(v,v)\quad \text{for } v \in H_x.
\]

If this restriction satisfies a local lower bound
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)|_{H_x} \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} g(x)|_{H_x},
\quad x\in \Omega,
\]
then:

- All invariants satisfy a local \(CD(\rho_{\mathrm{loc}},\infty)\) condition,
- All functional inequalities may be derived **without needing curvature in vertical directions**,  
- Degeneracies due to symmetry do not break analysis.

This is the horizontal curvature principle used throughout the YM stack.

---

# 31. Quotient Interpretation

Although not needed explicitly, it is conceptually helpful to interpret:

- \(H_x\) as the tangent to the quotient space \(M/\mathcal{G}\),
- Invariant functions as functions on the quotient,
- The curvature bound \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu|_{H_x}\ge\rho_{\mathrm{loc}} g|_{H_x}\) as a curvature bound for the induced geometry on the quotient.

In practice:

- The quotient \(M/\mathcal{G}\) may be singular (as in YM),  
- The horizontal calculus bypasses the need to work on the quotient itself,  
- All analysis can be carried out on \(M\) but restricted to invariant functions and their horizontal gradients.

---

# 32. Local-to-Global Strategy (Geometry Summary)

With the geometric foundation complete, the mass-gap analytic strategy can be stated generically:

1. Identify a small region \(\Omega\subset M\) where curvature is strictly positive on horizontals.
2. Verify this curvature bound is uniform and stable under perturbations.
3. Use continuity to extend curvature from a point (e.g. identity) to an entire ball.
4. Recognize that invariants have horizontal gradients → curvature bound applies directly.
5. Combine with Lyapunov drift, which controls behavior outside \(\Omega\), to obtain global FI.
6. Use those global FI to obtain spectral information about the diffusion generator.
7. Use OS theory to connect diffusion spectral gaps with physical spectral gaps.

Everything from Step 2 onward relies on the foundational geometry presented in this file.

---

# 33. Foundational Results Summary

We now summarize the conclusions of the full Geometry Foundations file:

### (1) Geometry  
- Finite-dimensional, complete Riemannian manifolds  
- Product geometry and curvature inheritance  
- Normal coordinates, Haar Jacobian, Ricci expansion  

### (2) Analysis  
- Gradient, Hessian, Laplacian  
- Gibbs measures  
- Self-adjoint diffusion generators  
- Dirichlet forms and semigroups  

### (3) Curvature  
- Ricci curvature and BE tensor  
- Full Bochner–BE identity  
- Curvature–dimension condition and functional consequences  

### (4) Symmetry  
- Group actions, vertical/horizontal splitting  
- Invariant observables have horizontal gradients  
- Restricted curvature applies exactly to invariants  

These are the mathematical prerequisites for every major theorem in Parts II–V of the Yang–Mills proof stack.

# 02 — Bakry–Émery Calculus  
## Part 1 / ?

# 1. Overview

This file develops the **full Bakry–Émery \(\Gamma\) and \(\Gamma_2\) calculus** in a form suitable for geometric–analytic control of Gibbs measures. It unifies the curvature machinery that later applies to lattice Yang–Mills.

We work on a smooth, connected, complete Riemannian manifold \((M,g)\) of finite dimension \(n\), with Riemannian volume \(d\mathrm{vol}_g\).  
A \(C^2\) potential  
\[
S:M\to\mathbb{R}, \quad S \in C^2(M)
\]
defines the Gibbs measure:
\[
d\mu = Z^{-1} e^{-S} \, d\mathrm{vol}_g, \quad Z = \int_M e^{-S} d\mathrm{vol}_g.
\]

The associated generator is:
\[
L f = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S,\nabla f\rangle.
\]

Our goal is to derive:

1. Carré du champ \(\Gamma\)  
2. Second carré du champ \(\Gamma_2\)  
3. Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity  
4. Curvature–dimension condition  
5. Functional consequences (Poincaré, LSI, hypercontractivity)

This is the analytic heart of the entire YM mass-gap framework.

---

# 2. The Generator and Carré du Champ

Given the diffusion generator
\[
L f = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S, \nabla f\rangle,
\]
the carré du champ is defined for smooth \(f,g\):

\[
\Gamma(f,g) := \frac12\big(L(fg) - f Lg - g Lf\big).
\]

A direct calculation yields:
\[
\Gamma(f,g) = \langle \nabla f,\nabla g\rangle_g.
\]

Thus:
\[
\Gamma(f) := \Gamma(f,f) = |\nabla f|^2.
\]

This operator encodes the “energy dissipation rate” of the semigroup \(P_t = e^{tL}\).

---

# 3. Preliminaries: Connection and Hessian

To derive \(\Gamma_2\), we require the Levi–Civita connection \(\nabla\), Hessian \(\nabla^2\), and Ricci tensor \(\mathrm{Ric}\).

For any \(f\in C^\infty(M)\),
\[
\nabla^2 f(X,Y) = X(Yf) - (\nabla_X Y)f.
\]

The Riemannian Laplacian satisfies:
\[
\Delta_g f = \mathrm{div}(\nabla f)
= \sum_{i=1}^n \left( \nabla_{e_i}\nabla f \right)(e_i),
\]
for any local orthonormal frame \(\{e_i\}\).

---

# 4. Second Carré du Champ \(\Gamma_2\)

Definition:
\[
\Gamma_2(f,g)
:= \frac12\Big( L \Gamma(f,g) - \Gamma(f, Lg) - \Gamma(g, Lf) \Big).
\]

We mostly need \(\Gamma_2(f)=\Gamma_2(f,f)\).

Expanding using definitions gives:

\[
\Gamma_2(f)
= \frac12\left( L|\nabla f|^2 - 2 \Gamma(f,Lf) \right).
\]

This identity captures the “second-order energy dissipation” under \(P_t\).

---

# 5. Bochner Identity (Geometric Component)

For \(f\in C^\infty(M)\), the geometric Bochner formula is:

\[
\frac{1}{2}\Delta_g |\nabla f|^2
= \|\nabla^2 f\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
+ \langle \nabla f, \nabla \Delta_g f\rangle
+ \mathrm{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f).
\]

This is purely geometric: no potential has yet appeared.

---

# 6. Full Bakry–Émery Identity

We now account for the potential \(S\).

Since:
\[
Lf = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S, \nabla f\rangle,
\]
we compute:

\[
\Gamma_2(f)
= \|\nabla^2 f\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
+ \mathrm{Ric}_g(\nabla f,\nabla f)
+ \nabla^2 S(\nabla f,\nabla f).
\]

Thus:
\[
\Gamma_2(f) = \|\nabla^2 f\|^2 + \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(\nabla f,\nabla f),
\]
with
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu = \mathrm{Ric}_g + \nabla^2 S.
\]

This identity explicitly decomposes convexity into:

- Intrinsic (Ricci) part  
- Extrinsic (potential) part  

---

# 7. Interpretation: Convexity of Entropy

\(\Gamma_2\)-bounds control the second derivative of the entropy along the semigroup:

Define:
\[
g_t := P_t f, \quad \Phi(t) := \int_M g_t \log g_t \, d\mu.
\]

One shows:
\[
\Phi''(t) = 2\int_M \Gamma_2(\log g_t)\, g_t \, d\mu.
\]

Thus a lower bound
\[
\Gamma_2(h) \ge \rho \Gamma(h)
\]
for all smooth \(h\) forces:
\[
\Phi''(t) \ge -2\rho\, \Phi'(t),
\]
leading to exponential entropy decay and functional inequalities.

---

# 8. Bakry–Émery Curvature Tensor

The BE tensor:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu = \mathrm{Ric}_g + \nabla^2 S
\]
plays the role of a general “curvature” of the diffusion.

Properties:

- Symmetric bilinear form at each point  
- Smooth in \(x\)  
- Represents the curvature of the weighted manifold \((M,g,\mu)\)  

A lower bound
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g
\]
provides uniform functional inequalities.

The Yang–Mills program exploits this by combining:

- \(\mathrm{Ric}_g>0\) from Haar geometry  
- Hessian of the Wilson action \(\nabla^2 S_W\ge 0\) in horizontal directions  
- Small negative perturbations from additional terms bounded in magnitude  
- Continuity to extend to a neighborhood

to obtain local horizontal BE curvature.

---

# 9. Curvature–Dimension Condition \(CD(\rho,\infty)\)

The condition:

\[
\Gamma_2(f) \ge \rho \Gamma(f)
\quad \forall f\in C^\infty(M)
\]
is equivalent to:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g.
\]

Important notes:

- This is *dimension-free* (hence \(\infty\)).  
- For finite dimension \(n\), stronger versions \(CD(\rho,n)\) exist but are not required here.  
- Yang–Mills employs \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) restricted to horizontal subspaces.

Consequences:

- Poincaré inequality  
- Log-Sobolev inequality  
- Hypercontractive semigroup  
- Spectral gap  
- Gaussian concentration  

All with constants depending only on \(\rho\).

---

# 10. Horizontal Curvature (Restricted BE Theory)

In the presence of a subbundle \(H_x \subset T_x M\):

- Gradients of functions in a given subalgebra lie inside \(H_x\)
- Only the restriction of \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu\) to \(H_x\) matters

Formally:

\[
\Gamma_2(f)(x) \ge \rho \Gamma(f)(x)
\quad \text{whenever}\quad
\nabla f(x)\in H_x.
\]

Equivalent to:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)|_{H_x} \ge \rho\, g(x)|_{H_x}.
\]

This principle is essential for YM curvature, because gauge directions have zero BE curvature; only horizontals matter for gauge-invariant observables.

---

# 02 — Bakry–Émery Calculus  
## Part 2 / ?

# 11. Semigroup Framework

Let \(P_t = e^{tL}\) be the Markov semigroup generated by  
\[
Lf = \Delta_g f - \langle \nabla S,\nabla f\rangle.
\]

For sufficiently regular \(f \ge 0\):

- \(P_t f \ge 0\)  
- \(\int P_t f \, d\mu = \int f \, d\mu\) (mass preservation)  
- \(P_t\) is a contraction on \(L^p(\mu)\) for all \(1 \le p \le \infty\)

The diffusion equation solved by \(g_t := P_t f\) is:
\[
\partial_t g_t = L g_t.
\]

These semigroup properties are the analytic vehicle through which curvature controls functional inequalities.

---

# 12. Entropy Dissipation and First Derivative

Consider the entropy functional:
\[
\Phi(t) := \int_M g_t \log g_t \, d\mu.
\]

Since \(\int g_t \, d\mu\) is constant, this is the relative entropy up to an additive constant.

Differentiate:
\[
\Phi'(t)
= \int_M (\partial_t g_t)\log g_t \, d\mu
= \int_M L g_t \cdot \log g_t \, d\mu.
\]

Using symmetry of \(L\) in \(L^2(\mu)\) and integration by parts:
\[
\Phi'(t)
= -\int_M \Gamma(g_t, \log g_t)\, d\mu.
\]

Using \(\Gamma(g_t,\log g_t) = \frac{|\nabla g_t|^2}{g_t}\),
\[
\Phi'(t)
= -\int_M \frac{|\nabla g_t|^2}{g_t}\, d\mu.
\]

Hence:

- \(\Phi'(t) \le 0\): entropy decays  
- \(\Phi'(t)=0\) iff \(g_t\) is constant (equilibrium)

This is the entropy dissipation principle.

---

# 13. Second Derivative and \(\Gamma_2\)

A central Bakry–Émery computation yields:
\[
\Phi''(t)
= 2\int_M \Gamma_2(h_t) \, g_t \, d\mu,
\]
where \(h_t := \log g_t\).

This formula encodes the curvature of the diffusion flow.

**Derivation (sketch):**

1. Start from:
   \[
   \Phi'(t) = -\int_M g_t \Gamma(h_t) \, d\mu.
   \]

2. Differentiate:
   \[
   \Phi''(t)
   = -\int_M (\partial_t g_t)\Gamma(h_t)\,d\mu
     -\int_M g_t \partial_t \Gamma(h_t)\,d\mu.
   \]

3. Use:
   - \(\partial_t g_t = L g_t\)  
   - \(\partial_t \Gamma(h_t) = 2\Gamma(h_t,Lh_t)+2\Gamma_2(h_t)\)

4. Several cancellations occur by integration by parts and symmetry of \(L\).  
   The remaining term is exactly \(2\int \Gamma_2(h_t)\,g_t\,d\mu\).

Thus curvature enters entropy evolution through \(\Gamma_2\).

---

# 14. Differential Inequality Under Curvature Bound

Assume the curvature–dimension condition
\[
\Gamma_2(u) \ge \rho \Gamma(u) \quad \forall u.
\]

Apply to \(u = h_t = \log g_t\):
\[
\Gamma_2(h_t) \ge \rho \Gamma(h_t).
\]

Multiply both sides by \(g_t\) and integrate:
\[
\int_M \Gamma_2(h_t)g_t \, d\mu
\ge \rho \int_M \Gamma(h_t)g_t \, d\mu.
\]

Recall:
\[
\Phi'(t) = -\int_M g_t \Gamma(h_t) d\mu.
\]

Thus:
\[
\Phi''(t)
= 2\int_M \Gamma_2(h_t)g_t d\mu
\ge -2\rho \Phi'(t).
\]

Hence the differential inequality:
\[
\Phi''(t) + 2\rho \Phi'(t) \ge 0.
\]

---

# 15. Exponential Decay of Entropy

Define:
\[
\Psi(t) := -\Phi'(t) \ge 0.
\]

The inequality becomes:
\[
\Psi'(t) + 2\rho \Psi(t) \le 0,
\]
or equivalently:
\[
\frac{d}{dt}\left( e^{2\rho t}\Psi(t)\right) \le 0.
\]

Thus:
\[
\Psi(t) \le e^{-2\rho t}\Psi(0).
\]

Integrating:
\[
\Phi(0) - \Phi(t)
= \int_0^t \Psi(s)\,ds
\le \Psi(0)\int_0^t e^{-2\rho s} ds
= \frac{1-e^{-2\rho t}}{2\rho}\Psi(0).
\]

Letting \(t\to\infty\) gives:
\[
\Phi(0) \le \frac{\Psi(0)}{2\rho},
\]
that is:
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f) \le \frac{1}{\rho}\int_M \frac{|\nabla f|^2}{f} \, d\mu.
\]

Specializing to \(f^2\) yields the usual log-Sobolev inequality.

---

# 16. Log-Sobolev Inequality (Precise Form)

Let \(f\in C^\infty(M)\) with \(\int f^2 d\mu = 1\).  
Then the LSI states:
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2)
\le \frac{2}{\rho} \int_M |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
\]

This inequality is **strictly stronger** than Poincaré and implies hypercontractivity.

---

# 17. Poincaré Inequality via Spectral Decomposition

The Poincaré inequality:
\[
\mathrm{Var}_\mu(f)
\le \frac{1}{\rho}\int_M |\nabla f|^2 d\mu
\]
follows from:

- The BE identity  
- The eigenfunction calculation:
  \[
  \lambda \ge \rho
  \]
  for every nonzero eigenvalue \(\lambda\) of \(-L\)

Thus \(\lambda_1\ge\rho\).

(Proof: integrate \(\Gamma_2(\varphi)\) for an eigenfunction \(L\varphi=-\lambda\varphi\).)

---

# 18. Hypercontractivity

Under LSI with constant \(C_{\mathrm{LS}} = 2/\rho\):

\[
\|P_t f\|_{L^q(\mu)} \le \|f\|_{L^p(\mu)},
\]
whenever
\[
q(t) - 1 = (p(t)-1)e^{2\rho t}.
\]

This describes smoothing of functions under the diffusion semigroup.

---

# 19. Gaussian Concentration

If \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g\) with \(\rho>0\), then any 1-Lipschitz function \(F\) satisfies:

\[
\mu\left( F - \mu(F) \ge r \right)
\le \exp\left( -\frac{\rho r^2}{2} \right).
\]

This strong concentration plays a conceptual role in understanding fluctuations in finite-volume YM systems.

---

# 20. Tensorization of BE Curvature

For product manifolds:
\[
M = M_1 \times \cdots \times M_k,
\quad
g = g_1 \oplus \cdots \oplus g_k,
\]
and potentials
\[
S = S_1 + \cdots + S_k,
\]
one has:

\[
\mathrm{Ric}_{\mu}(v,v)
= \sum_{i=1}^k \mathrm{Ric}_{\mu_i}(v_i,v_i).
\]

Lower bounds are inherited:

If
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_{\mu_i} \ge \rho g_i
\quad \forall i,
\]
then:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g.
\]

This guarantees **volume-uniform curvature** for product systems.

---

# END OF PART 2

File: 02_Bakry_Emery_Calculus.md (Part 3 / 3)
# 02 — Bakry–Émery Calculus  
## Part 3 / 3 (Final)

# 21. Local Curvature and Restricted Domains

Most interacting systems — certainly lattice Yang–Mills — do **not** satisfy a global curvature–dimension condition.  
Curvature is typically positive only in a **small region**, and may deteriorate or even become negative elsewhere.

Thus we require a refined notion:

> **Local curvature bound** on an open set \(\Omega \subset M\):  
> \[
> \Gamma_2(f)(x) \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}}\Gamma(f)(x)  
> \quad \forall x\in\Omega.
> \]

This is equivalent to:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)\big(v,v\big) \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} |v|^2
\quad \forall v\in T_x M.
\]

Later in Yang–Mills, this inequality is only available in a ball around the trivial configuration.

---

# 22. Restricted Curvature: Horizontal BE Condition

In gauge theories, curvature is **not** needed in the vertical (gauge) directions, because all gauge-invariant functions have gradients in the horizontal subspace \(H_x\).

Thus we adopt:

> **Horizontal curvature bound** on \(\Omega\):  
> \[
> \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)(v,v) \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} |v|^2,
> \quad \forall x\in\Omega, \ \forall v\in H_x.
> \]

This ensures:
\[
\Gamma_{2}(f)(x)
= \|\nabla^2 f(x)\|^2 + \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)(\nabla f(x),\nabla f(x))
\ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} \, \Gamma(f)(x)
\]
for all gauge-invariant \(f\).

### Key point:

Horizontal curvature + horizontal gradients = effective curvature–dimension condition for the physical sector.

This is precisely how the Yang–Mills curvature result becomes analytically usable.

---

# 23. Continuity of the Curvature Tensor

Finite-dimensional smoothness yields:

- \(\mathrm{Ric}_g(x)\) is smooth in \(x\)
- \(\nabla^2 S(x)\) is smooth in \(x\)
- \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)\) is smooth in \(x\)

Hence eigenvalues vary continuously:

If  
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x_0)|_{H_{x_0}} \ge \rho_0 > 0,
\]
then:
\[
\exists r>0\ \text{s.t.}\ 
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)|_{H_x} \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}}:=\rho_0/2
\ \forall x\in B_r(x_0).
\]

This is exactly the argument used to extend YM curvature from the trivial configuration \(U^{(0)}\) to a uniform small-field ball.

---

# 24. Negative Contributions and Potential Bounds

Interacting potentials often have negative Hessian directions.  
If the negative curvature directions are **controlled**:

\[
\nabla^2 S(x) \ge -C_{\mathrm{neg}} g(x),
\]
then one can still obtain positive curvature locally provided geometric curvature dominates:

\[
\mathrm{Ric}_g(x) - C_{\mathrm{neg}} g(x) \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} g(x).
\]

In Yang–Mills:

- \(\mathrm{Ric}_g = \mathrm{Ric}_{G^{E(\Lambda)}} \ge \kappa_G g\)
- Additional terms in the action have bounded negative Hessian
- Wilson Hessian is nonnegative in horizontals
- \(\kappa_G - C_{\mathrm{add}} > 0\) yields local positive curvature

---

# 25. Local Curvature → Local Functional Inequalities

By the BE calculus:

If \(CD(\rho_{\mathrm{loc}},\infty)\) holds on a bounded domain \(U\subset\Omega\), then:

- Local Poincaré inequality holds on \(U\):
  \[
  \int_U (f - f_U)^2 \, d\mu
  \le C_{\mathrm{P,loc}} \int_U |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
  \]

- Local log-Sobolev inequality holds on \(U\):
  \[
  \mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2 1_U)
  \le C_{\mathrm{LS,loc}} \int_U |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
  \]

Constants depend on geometry of \(U\), local curvature bound, and local normalization of \(\mu\), but **not on the global volume**.

These local FI form the inputs required for the local-to-global machinery.

---

# 26. Local Curvature + Lyapunov Drift → Global Inequalities

The Bakry–Émery calculus provides the **local analytic engine**.  
To extend inequalities to all of \(M\), one uses a Lyapunov function \(W\):

\[
LW \le -\alpha W + \beta 1_{K},
\]
with \(K\subset U \subset \Omega\).

The combination of:

1. Local curvature → local FI  
2. Drift inequality → control of tails  
3. Patchwork argument → absorb defect terms  

produces **global Poincaré and log-Sobolev inequalities**.

This is the analytic heart of Part III.

---

# 27. Spectral Consequences in Restricted Setting

If \(CD(\rho_{\mathrm{loc}},\infty)\) holds only for invariant observables, but global FI extends to the invariant subspace, then:

- The generator restricted to invariants has spectral gap  
- Convergence of the semigroup in the invariant sector is exponential  
- For YM, this is the sector that contains all physically meaningful observables

Thus horizontal curvature governs the physical spectral structure.

---

# 28. Why BE Calculus Works So Well for Gauge Theories

Gauge theories have:

- Large symmetry groups  
- Degenerate metric directions (verticals)  
- Potentials built from holonomies (highly nonlinear)  
- Curvature positive only near the identity  

Yet BE calculus overcomes these difficulties because:

1. BE curvature automatically incorporates the Hessian of the potential.  
2. Horizontal restriction extracts exactly the directions relevant for invariants.  
3. Continuity of curvature handles degeneracies in vertical directions.  
4. Tensorization ensures volume-independent constants.  
5. Local-curvature-plus-Lyapunov is the correct analytic tool for non-globally convex systems.

This calculus is the mathematical skeleton of the mass-gap analytic strategy.

---

# 29. Summary of the Bakry–Émery Calculus Module

This file establishes:

- The carré du champ and \(\Gamma_2\) operators  
- The Bochner–BE identity  
- The BE curvature tensor \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu\)  
- The curvature–dimension condition \(CD(\rho,\infty)\)  
- Functional inequalities derived from curvature  
- Entropy dissipation under the diffusion semigroup  
- Restricted (horizontal) curvature theory  
- Local curvature behavior and extensions by continuity  
- Local-to-global analytic mechanism foundation  

All later parts — especially Parts II and III — rely critically on these results.

---

# END OF FILE 02  
(Bakry–Émery Calculus — Complete)

# 03 — Curvature–Dimension → Poincaré & Log-Sobolev  
## Part 1 / ?

# 1. Purpose of This File

This file consolidates the complete derivation of **global Poincaré** and **logarithmic Sobolev inequalities** (LSI) from the **curvature–dimension condition** \(CD(\rho,\infty)\).

Unlike File 02 (which develops the Bakry–Émery calculus), this file focuses on the *functional inequality consequences*, making the logical chain completely explicit and ready for application to interacting systems such as lattice Yang–Mills.

We prove in detail:

1. \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) ⇒ spectral gap (Poincaré inequality)  
2. \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) ⇒ log-Sobolev inequality  
3. Hypercontractivity of \(P_t\)  
4. Relationships among PI, LSI, and semigroup decay  
5. Tensorization stability  
6. Restricted versions for invariant subspaces  

These are the analytic tools used in Parts III–V of the YM program.

---

# 2. Setting

Let \((M,g)\) be a finite-dimensional complete Riemannian manifold with Gibbs measure:
\[
d\mu = Z^{-1} e^{-S} d\mathrm{vol}_g,
\]
where \(S\in C^2(M)\).

Define generator:
\[
Lf = \Delta_g f - \langle\nabla S,\nabla f\rangle.
\]

Define carré du champ:
\[
\Gamma(f,g) = \langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle, \qquad \Gamma(f) = |\nabla f|^2.
\]

Define second carré du champ:
\[
\Gamma_2(f)
= \frac12 \big( L\Gamma(f) - 2\Gamma(f,Lf) \big).
\]

We assume the curvature-dimension condition:

> **\(CD(\rho,\infty)\)**:  
> \[
> \Gamma_2(f) \ge \rho\, \Gamma(f) \quad \forall f\in C^\infty(M)
> \]
> for some \(\rho>0\).

---

# 3. Spectral Theory and Dirichlet Forms

Let \(-L\) be the nonnegative self-adjoint operator associated to the Dirichlet form
\[
\mathcal{E}(f) = \int_M |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
\]

The spectral decomposition yields eigenpairs
\[
L\varphi_k = -\lambda_k \varphi_k, 
\qquad 0=\lambda_0 < \lambda_1 \le \lambda_2 \le \cdots.
\]

For any \(f\),
\[
f = \sum_{k=0}^\infty c_k \varphi_k,
\qquad 
\|f\|_{L^2(\mu)}^2 = \sum |c_k|^2,
\qquad
\mathcal{E}(f) = \sum \lambda_k |c_k|^2.
\]

The variance is:
\[
\mathrm{Var}_\mu(f) = \sum_{k\ge 1} |c_k|^2.
\]

To prove PI with constant \(1/\rho\), it suffices to prove:

\[
\lambda_1 \ge \rho.
\]

---

# 4. \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) ⇒ Eigenvalue Bound

Let \(\varphi\) be an eigenfunction with \(L\varphi = -\lambda \varphi\), \(\lambda>0\).

Compute:
\[
\int \Gamma_2(\varphi)\, d\mu
= -\int \Gamma(\varphi, L\varphi)\, d\mu
= \lambda \int \Gamma(\varphi)\, d\mu.
\]

Under \(CD(\rho,\infty)\):
\[
\int \Gamma_2(\varphi) d\mu
\ge \rho \int \Gamma(\varphi) d\mu.
\]

Hence:
\[
\lambda \int \Gamma(\varphi) d\mu
\ge \rho \int \Gamma(\varphi) d\mu.
\]

Since \(\varphi\) is nonconstant, \(\int\Gamma(\varphi)>0\), so:

\[
\boxed{\lambda \ge \rho}.
\]

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{\lambda_1 \ge \rho}.
\]

This proves the spectral gap and Poincaré inequality.

---

# 5. Poincaré Inequality

Given \(\lambda_1 \ge \rho\), for any \(f\):

\[
\mathrm{Var}_\mu(f)
= \sum_{k\ge1} |c_k|^2
\le \frac{1}{\rho}\sum_{k\ge1} \lambda_k |c_k|^2
= \frac{1}{\rho} \mathcal{E}(f).
\]

Thus:

\[
\boxed{
\mathrm{Var}_\mu(f)
\le \frac{1}{\rho}\int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
}
\]

This is the global Poincaré inequality of constant \(1/\rho\).

---

# 6. Logarithmic Sobolev Inequality

Let \(f\ge 0\), \(g_t = P_t f\), \(\Phi(t) = \int g_t \log g_t d\mu\).  
As shown in File 02:

\[
\Phi''(t) = 2\int \Gamma_2(\log g_t) g_t d\mu.
\]

Under \(CD(\rho,\infty)\):

\[
\Phi''(t) \ge -2\rho\,\Phi'(t).
\]

Letting \(\Psi(t)=-\Phi'(t)\ge0\):

\[
\Psi'(t) + 2\rho\,\Psi(t)\le0.
\]

Thus:
\[
\Psi(t) \le e^{-2\rho t}\Psi(0).
\]

Integrating:
\[
\Phi(0)-\Phi(t)
\le \frac{1-e^{-2\rho t}}{2\rho}\Psi(0).
\]

Sending \(t\to\infty\) and rewriting yields:

\[
\boxed{
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2) \le \frac{2}{\rho} \int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
}
\]

This is the LSI.

---

# 7. LSI ⇒ Hypercontractivity

Given LSI with constant \(C_{\mathrm{LS}}\), the semigroup satisfies:

\[
\|P_t f\|_{L^q(\mu)} \le \|f\|_{L^p(\mu)},
\]
provided
\[
q(t)-1 = (p-1) e^{2t/C_{\mathrm{LS}}}.
\]

Under \(CD(\rho,\infty)\), \(C_{\mathrm{LS}}=2/\rho\), so:

\[
q-1 = (p-1)e^{\rho t}.
\]

This “smoothing” effect is used conceptually in several proofs.

---

# 8. Stability Under Tensorization

If \((M_i, \mu_i)\) satisfy \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) with Gibbs potentials \(S_i\),  
then for the product:

\[
M = \prod_i M_i, \quad \mu = \prod_i \mu_i,
\]
with sum potential, one has:

\[
CD(\rho,\infty)
\quad \text{on } (M,\mu).
\]

Consequences:

- Global Poincaré constant is unchanged  
- Global LSI constant is unchanged  
- Curvature and FI are volume-independent  

This is essential for lattice applications.

---

# 9. Restricted PI/LSI for Subalgebras

If a subalgebra \(\mathcal{A}\subset C^\infty(M)\) satisfies:

- \(\nabla f(x)\in H_x\) for all \(f\in\mathcal{A}\),  
- Horizontal curvature bound:
  \[
  \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(x)|_{H_x} \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} g|_{H_x}
  \quad x\in\Omega,
  \]

then:

- A local PI/LSI holds on \(\Omega\) for functions in \(\mathcal{A}\)
- Combined with Lyapunov drift, this yields global PI/LSI on \(\mathcal{A}\)

This is the analytic translation of horizontal curvature in YM.

---

# 10. Practical Version of the CD→FI Pipeline

We summarize the full logical chain:

1. BE identity:
   \[
   \Gamma_2 = \|\nabla^2 f\|^2 + \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(\nabla f,\nabla f).
   \]

2. Lower bound of BE curvature:
   \[
   \mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g.
   \]

3. This implies:
   - Spectral gap \(\lambda_1 \ge \rho\)  
   - Poincaré inequality  
   - LSI  
   - Hypercontractivity  
   - Gaussian concentration  

4. Via tensorization, product systems inherit the same constants.

5. Via restricted curvature, symmetry-reduced sectors inherit the same constants.

This pipeline is used repeatedly throughout the YM proof structure.

---

# 11. The Importance of “Dimension-Free” Constants

The constant \(\rho\) in \(CD(\rho,\infty)\):

- Is **independent** of the dimension of \(M\)  
- Does **not degrade** as the number of factors grows  
- Therefore survives volume limits and refinement of discretization  
- Is ideal for application to Yang–Mills lattices of arbitrary size  

This property is crucial for the uniformity of the mass-gap argument.

---

# 12. Summary of the CD → PI/LSI Module

In this file we have proven:

1. \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) ⇒ spectral gap:  
   \[
   \lambda_1 \ge \rho.
   \]

2. \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) ⇒ Poincaré inequality:
   \[
   \mathrm{Var}(f) \le \frac{1}{\rho}\int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
   \]

3. \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) ⇒ LSI:
   \[
   \mathrm{Ent}(f^2) \le \frac{2}{\rho}\int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu.
   \]

4. Hypercontractivity follows from LSI.

5. FI and curvature bounds tensorize.

6. Horizontal curvature yields restricted FI for invariant observables.

These results form the analytic bridge from curvature theorems (Part II) to global functional inequalities (Part III).

---

# END OF FILE 03  
(Curvature–Dimension → PI/LSI — Complete)

# 04 — Gauge Geometry  
## Part 1 / ?

# 1. Purpose of This File

This file constructs the **geometric structure of gauge theories** on finite lattices in a mathematically rigorous, coordinate-free manner. It establishes:

- The lattice configuration manifold \(M_\Lambda = G^{E(\Lambda)}\)  
- The lattice gauge group \(\mathcal{G}_\Lambda = G^{V(\Lambda)}\)  
- The gauge action on configurations  
- Vertical (gauge) directions as tangent to orbits  
- Horizontal (physical) directions as orthogonal complements  
- Behavior of gradients of gauge-invariant observables  
- Gauge-equivariance of the decomposition  

This forms the geometric backbone for **horizontal curvature** (Part II) and **restricted functional inequalities** (Part III).

Unlike the Wilson action or curvature calculations, this file is purely **geometric** — independent of the form of the potential.

---

# 2. Lattice Configuration Manifold \(M_\Lambda\)

Let:

- \(\Lambda \subset \mathbb{Z}^d\) be a finite hypercubic lattice  
- \(E(\Lambda)\) be the set of oriented edges  
- \(G\) be a compact, connected Lie group (e.g. \(SU(N)\))  
- \(g_G\) be a bi-invariant Riemannian metric on \(G\)

Then define:

\[
M_\Lambda := G^{E(\Lambda)}.
\]

A configuration \(U\in M_\Lambda\) assigns to each edge \((x,\mu)\) a group element \(U_{x,\mu}\in G\).

## 2.1 Manifold Structure

As a finite Cartesian product of smooth manifolds:

\[
\dim M_\Lambda = |E(\Lambda)| \cdot \dim G.
\]

Local coordinates are obtained by exponential coordinates on each factor.

## 2.2 Product Riemannian Metric

Define:
\[
g_\Lambda(U)\big( (V_\ell),(W_\ell) \big)
= \sum_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)} g_G(U_\ell)(V_\ell, W_\ell),
\]
with right-invariant identification:
\[
T_{U_\ell}G \cong \mathfrak{g},\quad 
V_\ell = dR_{U_\ell}(X_\ell).
\]

Thus:
\[
|(V_\ell)|_{g_\Lambda}^2 = \sum_\ell |X_\ell|_G^2.
\]

The corresponding volume form is the product Haar measure:
\[
d\mathrm{vol}_{g_\Lambda}(U) = \prod_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)} dU_\ell.
\]

---

# 3. Lattice Gauge Group \(\mathcal{G}_\Lambda\)

Define the gauge group:
\[
\mathcal{G}_\Lambda := G^{V(\Lambda)}.
\]

Elements \(g = (g_x)\) assign a group element to each site.

## 3.1 Gauge Action on Link Variables

The action on \(M_\Lambda\) is:
\[
(g\cdot U)_{x,\mu}
= g_x\, U_{x,\mu}\, g_{x+\hat\mu}^{-1}.
\]

This corresponds to changing the local trivialization of the gauge bundle at each site.

The map:
\[
\mathcal{G}_\Lambda \times M_\Lambda \to M_\Lambda,\quad
(g,U)\mapsto g\cdot U,
\]
is smooth.

---

# 4. Gauge Action is Isometric

Since \(g_G\) is bi-invariant, left and right multiplication are isometries.

Thus each component map
\[
U_{x,\mu} \mapsto g_x U_{x,\mu} g_{x+\hat\mu}^{-1}
\]
is an isometry of \((G,g_G)\).

Therefore the full map
\[
U \mapsto g\cdot U
\]
is an isometry of \((M_\Lambda,g_\Lambda)\).

Consequences:

1. Riemannian norms are gauge-invariant:  
   \[
   |d(g\cdot)_U(V)|_{g_\Lambda} = |V|_{g_\Lambda}.
   \]

2. Haar volume is preserved:  
   \[
   d\mathrm{vol}_{g_\Lambda}(g\cdot U) = d\mathrm{vol}_{g_\Lambda}(U).
   \]

3. Gauge covariance intertwines with metric orthogonality.

---

# 5. Gauge Orbits

The orbit of a configuration \(U\) is:
\[
\mathcal{O}_U = \{ g\cdot U \mid g\in\mathcal{G}_\Lambda \}.
\]

This is an immersed submanifold of \(M_\Lambda\).  
Its dimension may drop at singular configurations (e.g., reducible fields), but on an open dense set (regular configurations) it has constant dimension.

We denote this regular set by:
\[
M_\Lambda^{\mathrm{reg}}.
\]

---

# 6. Vertical Subspace at \(U\)

At each \(U\in M_\Lambda\) define the **vertical subspace**:
\[
V_U := T_U \mathcal{O}_U.
\]

To compute it, take for each site \(x\) a Lie algebra element \(X_x\in\mathfrak{g}\), form the fundamental vector field corresponding to the one-parameter gauge transformation:

\[
g(t)_x = \exp(t X_x),
\]
and compute:
\[
X^\sharp (U)
= \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0} \big( g(t)\cdot U \big).
\]

Componentwise:
\[
(X^\sharp(U))_{x,\mu}
= X_x U_{x,\mu} - U_{x,\mu} X_{x+\hat\mu}.
\]

Thus:
\[
V_U = \{ X^\sharp(U) : X \in \mathfrak{g}_\Lambda \},
\quad \mathfrak{g}_\Lambda = \bigoplus_{x\in V(\Lambda)} \mathfrak{g}.
\]

Interpretation:

- Vertical directions = infinitesimal gauge transformations  
- Physically: zero-energy, unphysical deformations  

---

# 7. Horizontal Subspace at \(U\)

Define:
\[
H_U := V_U^\perp
\]
with respect to the metric \(g_\Lambda\).

Thus:
\[
T_U M_\Lambda = H_U \oplus V_U.
\]

This decomposition is orthogonal.

Properties:

1. \(H_U\) varies smoothly with \(U\) on \(M_\Lambda^{\mathrm{reg}}\).  
2. It transforms equivariantly:
   \[
   d(g\cdot)_U(H_U) = H_{g\cdot U}.
   \]
3. It contains all physically meaningful directions.

This is the canonical Riemannian horizontal distribution associated with the gauge action.

---

# 8. Gauge-Invariant Observables and Horizontal Gradients

A smooth function \(f : M_\Lambda \to \mathbb{R}\) is gauge-invariant if:
\[
f(g\cdot U) = f(U)
\quad \forall g\in\mathcal{G}_\Lambda.
\]

Let \(f\in C^\infty(M_\Lambda)\) be gauge-invariant.  
Then:

## 8.1 Differential Vanishes on Vertical Directions

For any vertical vector \(V\in V_U\):
\[
df_U(V) = 0.
\]

Reason: \(V\) is the derivative of a gauge transformation curve \(\gamma(t) = g(t)\cdot U\).  
Gauge invariance of \(f\) implies:
\[
f(\gamma(t)) = f(U) \implies df_U(\gamma'(0))=0.
\]

## 8.2 Gradient is Horizontal

By definition:
\[
\langle \nabla f(U),V\rangle_{g_\Lambda} = df_U(V) = 0
\quad \forall V\in V_U.
\]

Thus:
\[
\boxed{\nabla f(U)\in H_U}.
\]

This fact is used repeatedly in curvature and FI arguments.

---

# 9. Interpretation of Horizontal Geometry

- **Vertical directions** reproduce gauge redundancy.  
- **Horizontal directions** represent genuine degrees of freedom.  
- **Gauge-invariant observables only probe horizontal geometry.**

Later, the **horizontal restriction** of the Bakry–Émery tensor controls curvature:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)|_{H_U}.
\]

This restriction is what permits analysis on physical variables without gauge fixing.

---

# 10. Behavior Under Gauge Transformations

The gauge action satisfies:

\[
d(g\cdot)_U(V_U) = V_{g\cdot U},\qquad
d(g\cdot)_U(H_U) = H_{g\cdot U}.
\]

Therefore:

- Horizontal/vertical splitting is gauge-equivariant  
- Gradients of gauge-invariant observables transform covariantly  
- Ricci curvature, Hessians, and BE tensors transform appropriately under pushforward  

Thus all geometric objects used in BE curvature theory behave naturally under gauge symmetry.

---

# 11. Connection to Quotient Geometry

The quotient \(M_\Lambda / \mathcal{G}_\Lambda\) is typically singular, but the horizontal distribution \(H_U\) behaves as the tangent to the quotient at regular points.

Gauge invariants correspond to functions on this quotient.

The metric on horizontals descends to the quotient metric.

Curvature bounds restricted to horizontals correspond to curvature bounds on the quotient.

This viewpoint justifies the horizontal curvature arguments in analytic parts of the YM program.

---

# END OF PART 1

(Part 2 will introduce discrete combinatorial aspects, oriented edges, plaquettes, cochains, and the Hodge-theoretic interpretation of horizontal directions.)

# 04 — Gauge Geometry  
## Part 2 / ?

# 12. Discrete Lattice Structure Underlying Gauge Geometry

Gauge fields on a lattice carry a combinatorial structure inherited from the cubic graph.  
This structure is essential for defining holonomies, curvature, gauge transformations, and the physical–gauge decomposition.

Let:

- \(V(\Lambda)\): set of lattice sites  
- \(E(\Lambda)\): set of oriented edges  
- \(P(\Lambda)\): set of oriented plaquettes  

These define the cellular complex that will later support discrete exterior derivatives, Hodge decompositions, and curvature operators.

---

# 13. Oriented Edges and the Gauge Action

An oriented edge is denoted \((x,\mu)\), connecting site \(x\) to \(x+\hat\mu\).  
The corresponding link variable \(U_{x,\mu}\) transforms under a gauge transformation \(g=(g_x)\) as:

\[
(g\cdot U)_{x,\mu}
= g_x\, U_{x,\mu}\, g_{x+\hat\mu}^{-1}.
\]

This reproduces the local gauge principle:

- Left multiplication at source  
- Right multiplication at target  

which corresponds to the action of the gauge group on the fiber of a principal bundle discretized on the lattice.

---

# 14. Lie Algebra Representation of Vertical Directions

Let \(X=(X_x)\in\mathfrak{g}^{V(\Lambda)}\).  
Then the infinitesimal gauge action produces vertical vectors:

\[
(X^\sharp(U))_{x,\mu}
= X_x U_{x,\mu} - U_{x,\mu} X_{x+\hat\mu}.
\]

Under the right-invariant identification \(T_{U_{x,\mu}}G\simeq\mathfrak{g}\):

\[
(X^\sharp(U))_{x,\mu}
= \mathrm{Ad}_{U_{x,\mu}}^{-1}(X_x) - X_{x+\hat\mu},
\]
up to consistent left/right conventions.

Key observations:

1. Vertical vectors encode infinitesimal site-based conjugations.  
2. Vertical directions form a linear subspace \(V_U \subset T_U M_\Lambda\).  
3. \(V_U\) has the same dimension as the gauge group’s dimension, modulo stabilizers.

---

# 15. Gauge-Orbit Dimension and Stabilizers

The orbit \(\mathcal{O}_U = \{g\cdot U\}\) has dimension:

\[
\dim \mathcal{O}_U = \dim \mathcal{G}_\Lambda - \dim \mathrm{Stab}(U),
\]
where:
\[
\mathrm{Stab}(U) = \{ g : g\cdot U = U \}.
\]

- For *generic* fields, the stabilizer is trivial → full orbit dimension.  
- For *reducible* fields (e.g., constant fields), stabilizers may be nontrivial → lower-dimensional strata.

Later (in the Polarity/Stratification appendix), we note that singular strata have **Haar measure zero** and do not affect curvature or FI arguments.

---

# 16. Horizontal Subspaces in Discrete Gauge Theory

Recall:
\[
H_U = V_U^\perp.
\]

At a configuration \(U\), a tangent vector \(W=(W_\ell)\) lies in \(H_U\) iff:
\[
\sum_{(x,\mu)\in E(\Lambda)} 
\left\langle W_{x,\mu},\, X_x U_{x,\mu} - U_{x,\mu} X_{x+\hat\mu} \right\rangle_{g_G}
= 0
\quad \forall X=(X_x).
\]

Equivalently, horizontality imposes a type of discrete “covariant divergence-free” condition on \(W\).  
Details depend on left vs right action conventions, but the orthogonality condition is always canonical.

---

# 17. Gauge Covariance of the Decomposition

The gauge action \(U\mapsto g\cdot U\) satisfies:

- \(d(g\cdot)_U(V_U) = V_{g\cdot U}\)  
- \(d(g\cdot)_U(H_U) = H_{g\cdot U}\)

Thus:

\[
d(g\cdot)_U\big( T_U M_\Lambda \big)
= T_{g\cdot U} M_\Lambda,
\]
and this mapping preserves the vertical/horizontal splitting.

As a consequence:

- Horizontal geometry is **gauge-equivariant**,  
- Horizontal curvature is **gauge-invariant**,  
- All FI estimates in horizontals are automatically gauge-invariant.

---

# 18. Gauge Invariance of Observables

Let \(f: M_\Lambda \to \mathbb{R}\) be gauge-invariant:

\[
f(g\cdot U) = f(U).
\]

Differentiate:
\[
df_U(X^\sharp(U)) = 0\quad\forall X.
\]

Thus:
\[
\nabla f(U) \in H_U.
\]

This is not a mild convenience — it is **the** structural fact that makes curvature-based analysis feasible in gauge theories.

Because only horizontals matter, vertical curvature (which is typically zero or negative) does not break PI/LSI.

---

# 19. Holonomies and Geometry

Given a plaquette \(p=(x;\mu,\nu)\), define the plaquette holonomy:
\[
U_p 
= U_{x,\mu}\, U_{x+\hat\mu,\nu}\, U_{x+\hat\nu,\mu}^{-1}\, U_{x,\nu}^{-1}.
\]

This is the discrete curvature of the lattice gauge field.

Although holonomies belong to analysis of the Wilson action, the **geometric fact** relevant here is:

\[
U_p(g\cdot U)
= g_x\, U_p(U)\, g_x^{-1},
\]
so holonomies transform by conjugation at the basepoint.

Thus:

- Gauge invariants depend only on conjugation-invariant functions of holonomies.  
- Such invariants have gradients lying in horizontals.  
- The horizontal distribution is effectively the tangent to the space of gauge orbits modulo conjugation.

---

# 20. Horizontal Geometry as the Physical Geometry

The horizontal bundle captures:

- Dynamical variations of link fields modulo gauge  
- Directions in which the action sees physical variation  
- Directions in which curvature (Haar + Hessian) is positive

The quotient \(M_\Lambda / \mathcal{G}_\Lambda\) is non-manifold in general, with stratified singularities, but horizontal geometry avoids the need to work on the quotient explicitly.

Horizontal calculus allows:

\[
\Gamma(f) = |\nabla f|^2 = |\nabla^H f|^2,
\]
\[
\Gamma_2(f) = \|\nabla_H^2 f\|^2 + \mathrm{Ric}_\mu^H(\nabla f,\nabla f),
\]

where \(\nabla_H\) is the horizontal projection and  
\(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu^H := \mathrm{Ric}_\mu|_{H_U}\).

---

# 21. Why Gauge Geometry is Crucial for Curvature Analysis

Gauge symmetry prevents global elliptic coercivity:

- Vertical directions correspond to null directions of the action  
- Wilson Hessian has kernel including all pure gauge modes  
- Haar curvature does *not* repair vertical degeneracy  

Thus:

- Full curvature bound \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu \ge \rho g\) fails  
- But horizontal curvature bound \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu^H \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}} g^H\) **does hold** in small-field regions  

Given gauge-invariant observables satisfy \(\nabla f \in H_U\), horizontal curvature is *exactly* what is needed.

This simplifies the analysis dramatically while remaining fully rigorous.

---

# 04 — Gauge Geometry  
## Part 3 / ?

# 22. Discrete Cochain Complex and Its Role in Gauge Geometry

To understand horizontal directions and later relate them to the Wilson Hessian, we introduce the **discrete differential geometry** of the lattice.

The lattice is a finite cell complex with:

- 0-cells (sites)  
- 1-cells (oriented edges)  
- 2-cells (oriented plaquettes)  

This naturally gives rise to cochain spaces:

\[
\mathcal{C}^0(\Lambda;\mathfrak{g}) = \mathfrak{g}^{V(\Lambda)},
\quad
\mathcal{C}^1(\Lambda;\mathfrak{g}) = \mathfrak{g}^{E(\Lambda)},
\quad
\mathcal{C}^2(\Lambda;\mathfrak{g}) = \mathfrak{g}^{P(\Lambda)}.
\]

These are simply assignments of Lie algebra elements to sites, edges, and plaquettes.

---

# 23. Discrete Exterior Derivatives \(d_0\) and \(d_1\)

Define:

## 23.1 Site-to-Edge Derivative \(d_0 : \mathcal{C}^0 \to \mathcal{C}^1\)

For a 0-cochain \(\phi = (\phi_x)\),  
\[
(d_0 \phi)_{x,\mu} = \phi_{x+\hat\mu} - \phi_x.
\]

Interpretation: discrete gradient of a scalar (site-based) field.

## 23.2 Edge-to-Plaquette Derivative \(d_1 : \mathcal{C}^1 \to \mathcal{C}^2\)

For a 1-cochain \(X=(X_\ell)\),
\[
(d_1 X)_p = \sum_{\ell\in\partial p} \epsilon_{p,\ell}\, X_\ell,
\]
with \(\epsilon_{p,\ell} = \pm 1\) depending on orientation.

Interpretation: discrete curl of a vector (edge-based) field.

These operators satisfy the cochain property:
\[
d_1 d_0 = 0.
\]

---

# 24. Hodge Decomposition for Lattice 1-Cochains

On a finite lattice with inner products on cochains, one has:

\[
\mathcal{C}^1 = \mathrm{im}(d_0) \oplus \ker(\Delta_1) \oplus \mathrm{im}(d_1^*),
\]
where the 1-form Laplacian is:
\[
\Delta_1 = d_1^* d_1 + d_0 d_0^*.
\]

Thus any edge field decomposes uniquely into:

1. **Exact component** (pure gauge): \(\mathrm{im}(d_0)\)  
2. **Harmonic component**: \(\ker(\Delta_1)\)  
3. **Co-exact component**: \(\mathrm{im}(d_1^*)\)

This decomposition plays a central role in:

- Wilson Hessian analysis  
- Identification of physical positivity  
- Understanding horizontal directions

---

# 25. Relation Between Vertical Subspace and \(\mathrm{im}(d_0)\)

Vertical directions correspond to infinitesimal gauge transformations.

A gauge transformation is specified by \(X_x \in \mathfrak{g}\) at each site.  
Under right-invariant identification, the vertical tangent vector at an edge \((x,\mu)\) is:

\[
(X^\sharp(U))_{x,\mu}
= X_x - \mathrm{Ad}_{U_{x,\mu}}(X_{x+\hat\mu}).
\]

Near the identity (in exponential coordinates) this linearizes to:

\[
(X^\sharp(U^{(0)}))_{x,\mu}
= X_x - X_{x+\hat\mu}
= -(d_0 X)_{x,\mu}.
\]

Thus:

\[
V_{U^{(0)}} \simeq \mathrm{im}(d_0)
\]
in the linearized coordinate representation.

This is the discrete analogue of vertical = exact 1-forms.

---

# 26. Horizontal Directions as Orthogonal Complement of \(\mathrm{im}(d_0)\)

Using Hodge decomposition:

\[
\mathcal{C}^1 = \mathrm{im}(d_0) \oplus \ker(\Delta_1) \oplus \mathrm{im}(d_1^*).
\]

Vertical directions correspond to \(\mathrm{im}(d_0)\).  
Thus horizontal directions correspond to:

\[
H_{U^{(0)}}
\simeq \ker(\Delta_1) \oplus \mathrm{im}(d_1^*).
\]

- \(\ker(\Delta_1)\): harmonic edge modes (global degrees of freedom depending on topology)  
- \(\mathrm{im}(d_1^*)\): co-exact 1-forms (local physical modes)

**Crucial implication:**

\[
\nabla f(U)\in H_U 
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\nabla f(U^{(0)}) \in \ker(\Delta_1) \oplus \mathrm{im}(d_1^*).
\]

This is the geometric origin of why the Wilson Hessian is strictly positive **only** on the co-exact component.

---

# 27. Adjoints and Metric Compatibility

The inner product on \(\mathcal{C}^1\) induced by \(g_\Lambda\) is:

\[
\langle X,Y\rangle_1 
= \sum_{\ell\in E(\Lambda)} \langle X_\ell, Y_\ell \rangle_G.
\]

Adjoints are defined by:

\[
\langle d_0 \phi, X \rangle_1 = \langle \phi, d_0^* X \rangle_0,
\]
\[
\langle d_1 X, Y \rangle_2 = \langle X, d_1^* Y \rangle_1.
\]

Gauge invariance of \(g_G\), and thus of \(g_\Lambda\), ensures all these operators behave naturally under gauge transformations.

---

# 28. Horizontal Tangent Space as “Physical Modes”

Summarizing the linear picture at \(U^{(0)}\):

\[
T_{U^{(0)}} M_\Lambda 
\cong \mathcal{C}^1
= \underbrace{\mathrm{im}(d_0)}_{\text{vertical}}
\ \oplus\
\underbrace{\ker(\Delta_1)}_{\text{harmonic}}
\ \oplus\
\underbrace{\mathrm{im}(d_1^*)}_{\text{physical}},
\]

Vertical directions ≅ pure gauge  
Harmonic directions ≅ global torons  
Co-exact directions ≅ propagating physical modes

Horizontal directions are:

\[
H_{U^{(0)}} 
= \ker(\Delta_1)\oplus\mathrm{im}(d_1^*).
\]

Later, when computing the Wilson Hessian:

\[
\nabla^2 S_W(U^{(0)}) = 2c_W\, d_1^* d_1,
\]
we find:

- Zero on \(\ker(\Delta_1)\) and \(\mathrm{im}(d_0)\)  
- Strictly positive on \(\mathrm{im}(d_1^*)\)

which aligns perfectly with the horizontal decomposition.

---

# 29. Behavior Away from the Identity

For general \(U\), the decomposition is more subtle because:

- Vertical subspaces \(V_U\) depend on \(U\)  
- Horizontal subspaces \(H_U\) depend on \(U\)  
- Cochain representation is no longer exact  

But gauge-equivariance ensures:

- \(H_{g\cdot U} = d(g\cdot)_U(H_U)\)  
- Vertical spaces always correspond to gauge orbits  
- Horizontal = \((\text{vertical})^\perp\) remains consistent

Thus the **physical picture is identical**, though the decomposition is not globally linear.

---

# 30. Holonomy Variations and Effective Lie Algebra Representation

Holonomy variations under infinitesimal edge deformations are given by:

\[
\delta U_{x,\mu} = U_{x,\mu} X_{x,\mu}
\quad\text{or}\quad
\delta U_{x,\mu} = X_{x,\mu} U_{x,\mu}
\]

depending on right- or left-invariant conventions.

Physical variations correspond to those which produce nontrivial changes in plaquette holonomies:
\[
\delta U_p = \sum_{\ell\in\partial p} \epsilon_{p,\ell} \delta U_\ell.
\]

In cochain language, holonomy variation is controlled by \(d_1\).  

This links horizontal directions to co-exact 1-forms, setting up the Wilson Hessian analysis in the next file.

---

# 04 — Gauge Geometry  
## Part 4 / 4 (Final)

# 31. Horizontal Geometry and the Effective Action

The physical analysis of gauge theories must restrict attention to **gauge-invariant observables**. Since their gradients lie entirely in \(H_U\), only the behavior of the action and curvature on horizontal directions is analytically relevant.

Let the effective action be
\[
S_\Lambda(U) = S_W(U) + S_{\mathrm{add},\Lambda}(U).
\]

The gradient \(\nabla S_\Lambda(U)\) lives in the full tangent space, but its effect on gauge-invariant observables enters **only through its horizontal projection**.  

Similarly:

- Hessians  
- Ricci curvature  
- BE curvature  
- Diffusion generator  
- Carré du champ operators  

must be examined on horizontals for consistency with gauge invariance.

---

# 32. Restricted Hessian Along Horizontal Directions

For gauge-invariant \(f\),
\[
\nabla f(U)\in H_U.
\]

Thus, when computing:
\[
\Gamma_2(f)(U)
= \|\nabla^2 f(U)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
+ \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)(\nabla f, \nabla f),
\]
only the restriction
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)|_{H_U}
\]
matters.

The vertical block of the Hessian contributes nothing because gradients of invariants have no vertical components.

Thus the analytic curvature condition simplifies from:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)\ge \rho g(U)
\]
(which fails in gauge theory)

to the effective physical condition:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)|_{H_U} \ge \rho_{\mathrm{loc}}\, g(U)|_{H_U}.
\]

This restricted condition **does hold** in lattice Yang–Mills in a small-field region.

---

# 33. The Horizontal Bakry–Émery Tensor

Define the horizontal BE tensor:
\[
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu^H(U)(v,w)
= \mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)(v,w)
\quad \text{for } v,w \in H_U.
\]

Its lower bound is defined by:
\[
\lambda_{\min}^H(U)
:= \inf_{\substack{v\in H_U \\ |v|=1}}
\mathrm{Ric}_\mu(U)(v,v).
\]

Theorems in Part II show:

- \(\lambda_{\min}^H(U^{(0)}) > 0\)  
- By continuity, \(\lambda_{\min}^H(U) > 0\) for all \(U\) in a small ball  
- The radius and lower bound are **independent of lattice volume**

This provides the **local horizontal curvature** required for functional inequalities.

---

# 34. Invariant Observables and the Effective Generator

The diffusion generator
\[
L_\Lambda f = \Delta_{g_\Lambda} f - \langle \nabla S_\Lambda, \nabla f\rangle
\]
when restricted to gauge invariants acts as:
\[
L_\Lambda^{\mathrm{inv}} f
= \Delta_{H} f - \langle \nabla^{H} S_\Lambda, \nabla^H f\rangle.
\]

Thus:

- All vertical components of \(L_\Lambda\) vanish on \(f\)  
- Only the horizontal Laplacian and horizontal gradient contribute  
- The semigroup \(P_t^{\mathrm{inv}}\) is generated by \(L_\Lambda^{\mathrm{inv}}\)

Therefore, the induced diffusion on the “physical” manifold is governed entirely by horizontal geometry.

---

# 35. Gauge Equivariance Ensures Consistency of Curvature Bounds

Because the horizontal bundle transforms equivariantly:
\[
H_{g\cdot U} = d(g\cdot)_U(H_U),
\]
a curvature bound at \(U^{(0)}\) automatically propagates to the entire gauge orbit of the local region around \(U^{(0)}\).

Thus no gauge-fixing is required to establish curvature bounds.

Gauge-fixing would break the natural geometry; the horizontal decomposition preserves it.

---

# 36. Behavior of Holonomies Under Horizontal Perturbations

Under a horizontal tangent vector \(W\in H_U\), the variation of plaquette holonomies is:

\[
\delta U_p = dU_p(U)[W].
\]

The key geometric fact is:

- Pure gauge variations (vertical) do **not** change any holonomies.  
  \[
  dU_p(U)[V]=0, \quad V\in V_U.
  \]

- Horizontal variations do change holonomies.  

Thus horizontals represent the physically relevant directions for the Wilson action:
\[
S_W(U) = \beta \sum_p \left( 1 - \frac{1}{N}\Re \operatorname{Tr} U_p \right).
\]

This prepares the stage for analyzing the Hessian of \(S_W\) on horizontals (File 05).

---

# 37. The Physical Meaning of the Horizontal Bundle

One may interpret:

- \(H_U\) as the tangent space to the **space of gauge equivalence classes** at point \([U]\)  
- The quotient space \(M_\Lambda / \mathcal{G}_\Lambda\) as the physical configuration space  
- Gauge invariants as functions on the quotient

Even though the quotient space is singular, horizontal calculus allows all differential geometry to be done cleanly on \(M_\Lambda\).

This is essential for:

- Curvature analysis  
- Functional inequalities  
- Hessian positivity of the Wilson action  
- Derivation of the mass gap

---

# 38. Summary of the Gauge Geometry Module

This file has built the complete geometric structure required for the analytic and curvature steps in the Yang–Mills mass-gap program:

### (1) Manifolds
- \(M_\Lambda = G^{E(\Lambda)}\) with product metric  
- Haar volume as Riemannian measure  

### (2) Symmetry

- Gauge group \(\mathcal{G}_\Lambda\) acts isometrically  
- Vertical = gauge directions  
- Horizontal = orthogonal complement, gauge-equivariant  

### (3) Calculus

- Gradients of gauge invariants lie in horizontals  
- BE curvature restricted to horizontals governs invariant observables  
- Holonomies vary only in horizontal directions  

### (4) Structure

- Linearization identifies horizontals with \(\ker(\Delta_1)\oplus \mathrm{im}(d_1^*)\)  
- Wilson Hessian positivity aligns with horizontal co-exact sector  
- Horizontal curvature forms the key input to local PI/LSI

These results make it possible to combine:

- Haar curvature  
- Wilson Hessian  
- Local curvature arguments  
- Lyapunov drift  

in a uniformly controlled way across all lattice volumes.

---
