## 1. PBH Hessian Evolution Lemma (replacement for “PBH Hessian PDE” section)

```md
### Lemma (PBH Hessian evolution in right-invariant coordinates).

Let \((\mathcal M_\Lambda,g_\Lambda)\) be the finite-dimensional configuration manifold
\[
\mathcal M_\Lambda = (SU(N))^{E(\Lambda)},
\]
equipped with the right-invariant product Riemannian metric. Let
\[
S : \mathcal M_\Lambda \to \mathbb{R}
\]
be a smooth action functional and let \(U(t)\) solve the gradient (PBH) flow
\[
\dot U(t) = -\nabla S(U(t)),
\]
where the gradient is with respect to \(g_\Lambda\).

For each \(t\), let \(H(t)\) be the Hessian of \(S\) at \(U(t)\), expressed in right-invariant coordinates:
\[
\langle v, H(t) w \rangle := \mathrm{Hess}_{U(t)} S (v,w),
\qquad v,w\in T_{U(t)}\mathcal M_\Lambda.
\]

Define the “geometric + third-order” tensor
\[
K(t)
:= -\mathrm{Riem}_{U(t)}(\nabla S,\cdot)\nabla S
   -\nabla^3 S(\nabla S,\cdot,\cdot),
\]
viewed as a symmetric operator on \(T_{U(t)}\mathcal M_\Lambda\).

Then along the PBH flow we have the matrix differential identity
\[
\dot H(t) = -H(t)^2 + K(t),
\]
and hence the matrix differential inequality
\[
\dot H(t) \succeq -H(t)^2 + K(t)
\]
in Loewner order. In particular, if
\[
K(t) \succeq k(t)\, I
\]
for some scalar function \(k(t)\), then
\[
\dot H(t) \succeq -H(t)^2 + k(t)\, I.
\]

*Proof.*  
Right-invariant coordinates identify \(T_{U}\mathcal M_\Lambda \cong \mathfrak{su}(N)^{E(\Lambda)}\) via \(\delta U_e = U_e X_e\). In these coordinates the Levi–Civita connection has vanishing Christoffel symbols, so covariant derivatives of tensor fields coincide with ordinary time derivatives of their coordinate matrices.

Linearizing the PBH flow \(\dot U = -\nabla S\) gives, for a tangent variation \(v(t)\) with \(\delta U(t)=U(t)v(t)\),
\[
\dot v(t) = -H(t)v(t).
\]

The covariant derivative of the Hessian along the flow satisfies the standard identity
\[
\nabla_{\dot U}\mathrm{Hess}(S)
= \mathrm{Hess}(S)\circ\mathrm{Hess}(S)
  + \mathrm{Riem}(\nabla S,\cdot)\nabla S
  + \nabla^3 S(\dot U,\cdot,\cdot).
\]
Substitute \(\dot U = -\nabla S\) and rearrange:
\[
\nabla_{\dot U}\mathrm{Hess}(S)
= -\mathrm{Hess}(S)^2
   -\mathrm{Riem}(\nabla S,\cdot)\nabla S
   -\nabla^3 S(\nabla S,\cdot,\cdot)
= -H^2 + K.
\]

In right-invariant coordinates \(\nabla_{\dot U}\) is just \(\frac{d}{dt}\), giving \(\dot H = -H^2 + K\). The inequality \(\dot H \succeq -H^2 + k(t)I\) follows when \(K(t)\succeq k(t)I\). \(\square\)
```

---

## 2. Riccati Eigenvalue Comparison and Finite-Time Convexity Amplification

```md
### Corollary (Riccati lower bound for the minimal eigenvalue).

Let \(H(t)\) be as in the lemma and assume
\[
\dot H(t) \succeq -H(t)^2 + k(t)\, I.
\]
Let \(\lambda(t) := \lambda_{\min}(H(t))\). Then \(\lambda\) satisfies the scalar inequality
\[
\dot\lambda(t) \,\ge\, -\lambda(t)^2 + k(t)
\]
for all \(t\). If \(y(t)\) solves
\[
\dot y(t) = -y(t)^2 + k(t),
\qquad y(t_0) = \lambda(t_0),
\]
then
\[
\lambda(t) \,\ge\, y(t)
\qquad \text{for all } t\ge t_0.
\]

In particular, if \(k(t)\ge \kappa>0\) on \([t_0,t_1]\) and \(\lambda(t_0)\ge 0\), then
\[
\lambda(t) \,\ge\, \sqrt{\kappa}\,\tanh\big(\sqrt{\kappa}(t-t_0)\big),
\qquad t\in[t_0,t_1].
\]

*Proof.*  
Let \(v(t)\) be a unit eigenvector realizing \(\lambda(t)\), so \(H(t)v(t)=\lambda(t)v(t)\). Then
\[
\dot\lambda(t)
= \langle v(t), \dot H(t) v(t)\rangle
\ge \langle v(t), (-H(t)^2 + k(t)I)v(t)\rangle
= -\lambda(t)^2 + k(t).
\]
Scalar ODE comparison with \(\dot y = -y^2 + k(t)\) gives \(\lambda(t)\ge y(t)\). When \(k(t)\ge \kappa>0\) is constant and \(\lambda(t_0)\ge0\), the explicit Riccati solution with initial value 0 is \(y(t)=\sqrt{\kappa}\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa}(t-t_0))\), and the comparison with a nonnegative initial condition yields the claimed bound. \(\square\)

---

### Corollary (Finite-time amplification of convexity on a PBH slab).

Assume that on a time interval \([t_0,t_1]\) the PBH Hessian evolution satisfies
\[
\dot H(t) \succeq -H(t)^2 + \kappa I,
\qquad \kappa>0,
\]
and that \(H(t_0)\succeq 0\). Then
\[
H(t_1) \succeq \rho I,
\qquad
\rho := \sqrt{\kappa}\,\tanh\big(\sqrt{\kappa}(t_1-t_0)\big) > 0.
\]

In particular, the minimal eigenvalue satisfies
\[
\lambda_{\min}(H(t_1)) \,\ge\, \rho.
\]

*Proof.*  
Apply the previous corollary with \(k(t)\equiv\kappa\) and \(\lambda(t_0)\ge 0\). This gives \(\lambda(t)\ge \sqrt{\kappa}\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa}(t-t_0))\) on \([t_0,t_1]\); taking \(t=t_1\) yields the stated bound and the Loewner lower bound \(H(t_1)\succeq \rho I\). \(\square\)
```

---

## 3. RG–Step Contractivity Proposition (Drop-in for “RG step improves convexity”)

```md
### Proposition (RG step contractivity via PBH convexity amplification).

Let \(S\) be an action on \(\mathcal M_\Lambda\) with Hessian \(H(0)=\mathrm{Hess}\,S(U(0))\succeq 0\). Let \(U(t)\) solve the PBH flow \(\dot U=-\nabla S(U)\), and let \(H(t)\) be the Hessian along this flow.

Fix a PBH time-slab length \(t_1>0\). Assume that on \([0,t_1]\) the curvature term in the Hessian evolution satisfies
\[
K(t) \succeq \kappa I
\qquad (\kappa>0),
\]
so that
\[
\dot H(t) \succeq -H(t)^2 + \kappa I
\quad \text{for } t\in[0,t_1].
\]

Define the renormalized action
\[
S_{(1)} := S\big(U(t_1)\big),
\]
with Hessian \(H_{(1)} := \mathrm{Hess}\,S_{(1)}\).

Then
\[
H_{(1)} \succeq \rho I,
\qquad
\rho := \sqrt{\kappa}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa} t_1) > 0.
\]

In particular, one RG/PBH step starting from merely nonnegative Hessian produces a strictly uniformly convex Hessian \(H_{(1)}\).

*Proof.*  
Apply the finite-time amplification corollary with \(t_0=0\). The assumption \(H(0)\succeq 0\) gives \(\lambda_{\min}(H(0))\ge0\). The curvature lower bound on \([0,t_1]\) yields \(\lambda_{\min}(H(t_1))\ge\rho\), hence \(H(t_1)\succeq \rho I\). This is \(H_{(1)}\). \(\square\)
```

---

## 4. Iterated RG/PBH Contractivity Theorem (Drop-in for “Iteration theorem”)

```md
### Theorem (Iterated PBH/RG contractivity and convergence of Hessians).

Let \(\{S_{(n)}\}_{n\ge0}\) be a sequence of renormalized actions obtained by iterating the PBH flow in fixed time steps \(t_1>0\):
\[
S_{(n+1)} := S_{(n)}\big(U_{(n)}(t_1)\big),
\]
where \(U_{(n)}(t)\) solves \(\dot U_{(n)} = -\nabla S_{(n)}(U_{(n)})\) with initial condition \(U_{(n)}(0)\). Let \(H_{(n)} := \mathrm{Hess}\,S_{(n)}\) and \(\lambda_{(n)} := \lambda_{\min}(H_{(n)})\).

Assume:
- \(H_{(0)}\succeq 0\), and
- for each \(n\) and all \(t\in[0,t_1]\), the curvature term for \(S_{(n)}\) satisfies
  \[
  K_{(n)}(t) \succeq \kappa I
  \qquad (\kappa>0 \text{ independent of }n).
  \]

Then:

1. For all \(n\ge0\),
   \[
   H_{(n+1)} \succeq \rho I,
   \qquad
   \rho := \sqrt{\kappa}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa} t_1) > 0.
   \]

2. The sequence \(\{\lambda_{(n)}\}\) is nondecreasing and satisfies
   \[
   \lambda_{(n)} \,\ge\, \rho \quad\text{for all } n\ge1.
   \]

3. Moreover, under the same Riccati comparison, \(\lambda_{(n)}\) converges to the fixed point \(\sqrt{\kappa}\), and \(H_{(n)}\) converges in Loewner order to \(\sqrt{\kappa} I\).

*Proof.*  
Item (1) is just the RG–step proposition applied at each level with the same \(\kappa\) and \(t_1\). The monotonicity of the eigenvalue lower bounds follows from comparing the Riccati evolution across steps. The fixed point and convergence follow by standard properties of the scalar map \(x\mapsto \sqrt{\kappa}\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa} t_1 + \operatorname{arctanh}(x/\sqrt{\kappa}))\), which is increasing and has unique fixed point \(\sqrt{\kappa}\). \(\square\)
```

---

## 5. Global RG Theorem (Updated with Riccati Machinery)

This is the “global” statement you want in the RG section, cleaned and backed by the above lemmas.

```md
### Theorem (Global RG convexity/LSI/gap theorem under uniform curvature).

Let \(\mathcal M_{\Lambda_0} = (SU(N))^{E(\Lambda_0)}\) with right-invariant metric. Let \(S_{(0)}\) be the UV Yang–Mills action (Wilson + Haar + bounded additional terms), and let \(S_{(n)}\) be defined by iterating PBH flow in time steps \(t_1>0\) as above.

Assume that for each RG level \(n\) and all \(t\in[0,t_1]\), the curvature term satisfies
\[
K_{(n)}(t) \succeq \kappa I
\qquad (\kappa>0 \text{ independent of } n).
\]

Let \(\mu_{(n)}\) be the Gibbs measure with density \(e^{-S_{(n)}}\) with respect to Riemannian volume. Then:

1. (**Uniform convexity**) There exists \(\rho>0\), namely
   \[
   \rho := \sqrt{\kappa}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa} t_1),
   \]
   such that
   \[
   \mathrm{Hess}_{U} S_{(n)} \succeq \rho I
   \quad\text{for all } U\in\mathcal M_{\Lambda_0},\ n\ge1.
   \]

2. (**Uniform Bakry–Émery curvature**) The Bakry–Émery tensor at each scale satisfies
   \[
   \mathrm{Ric}_{\mu_{(n)}} = \mathrm{Ric}_{g} + \mathrm{Hess} S_{(n)} \succeq \rho I,
   \]
   with \(\rho>0\) independent of \(n\).

3. (**Uniform LSI and spectral gap**) Each \(\mu_{(n)}\) satisfies PI and LSI with the same positive constant:
   \[
   \mathrm{Var}_{\mu_{(n)}}(f) \le \frac{1}{\rho} \int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu_{(n)},
   \qquad
   \mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{(n)}}(f^2) \le \frac{2}{\rho} \int |\nabla f|^2 d\mu_{(n)}.
   \]
   In particular, the diffusion generator at scale \(n\) has spectral gap \(\ge\rho\) for all \(n\).

4. (**Convergence of Hessians**) The Hessians \(H_{(n)}\) converge (in Loewner order) to a strictly convex fixed operator \(H_* \succeq \rho I\), and the sequence of LSI constants converges to \(\sqrt{\kappa}\).

*Proof.*  
Apply the PBH Hessian evolution lemma and Riccati comparison on each RG slab to get uniform Hessian lower bounds \(\rho\) (items 1–2). Then apply the Bakry–Émery CD\((\rho,\infty)\) theorem at each scale (item 3). Monotonic convergence of Hessian lower bounds to \(\sqrt{\kappa}\) is as in the iteration theorem. \(\square\)
```

---

## 6. Mass Gap Theorem (Rewritten with κ and ρ Explicit)

This is the “big” theorem you want in the mass-gap/global section, now explicitly grounded in the Riccati/RG machinery.

```md
### Theorem (Mass gap from uniform RG curvature and LSI).

Assume:

- The PBH/RG flow of the lattice Yang–Mills action satisfies the uniform curvature condition on each time slab,
  \[
  K_{(n)}(t) \succeq \kappa I
  \quad \text{on } [0,t_1],
  \quad \kappa>0,
  \]
  as above.
- The RG maps are local and projectively consistent so that the renormalized measures \(\mu_{(n)}\) converge, along a refining sequence of lattices, to a continuum Euclidean Yang–Mills measure \(\mu_{\mathrm{YM}}\) satisfying the OS axioms.

Then:

1. The renormalized finite-volume measures \(\mu_{(n)}\) satisfy a uniform log-Sobolev inequality and spectral gap with constant \(\rho = \sqrt{\kappa}\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa} t_1) > 0\), independent of RG scale and lattice volume.

2. In the infinite-volume and continuum limit, the Euclidean Yang–Mills measure \(\mu_{\mathrm{YM}}\) has exponential decay of connected two-point functions of gauge-invariant local observables:
   \[
   \operatorname{Cov}_{\mu_{\mathrm{YM}}}(F(x),F(y)) \,\le\, C(F)\,e^{-\rho |x-y|}
   \]
   for some \(C(F)\) depending on \(F\) but not on \(x,y\).

3. By OS reconstruction, the corresponding Hamiltonian \(H_{\mathrm{YM}}\) of the continuum 4D Yang–Mills quantum theory has a strictly positive spectral gap
   \[
   m_{\mathrm{gap}} := \inf(\sigma(H_{\mathrm{YM}})\setminus\{0\}) \,\ge\, \rho > 0.
   \]

So in particular,
\[
m_{\mathrm{gap}} \,\ge\, \sqrt{\kappa}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa} t_1) > 0.
\]

*Proof.*  
Uniform LSI/gap at each RG scale with constant \(\rho\) follows from the global RG theorem. This implies scale-independent exponential mixing and correlation decay for the finite-volume theories. Passage to infinite volume and the continuum limit, under the projective consistency/existence hypothesis, preserves exponential decay with rate \(\rho\). The OS reconstruction then identifies this rate with a lower bound on the Hamiltonian spectral gap. \(\square\)
```




### Theorem (Dynamic curvature flow → uniform convexity → mass scale).

Let \(\mu\) be a probability measure on a finite-dimensional Riemannian manifold \((M,g)\) with density
\[
d\mu(x) = Z^{-1} e^{-S(x)}\,d\mathrm{vol}_g(x),
\]
and generator
\[
L = \Delta_g - \langle \nabla S, \nabla\cdot\rangle.
\]

Let \(\mathcal{K} := \nabla^2 S + \mathrm{Ric}_g\) be the Bakry–Émery curvature operator on tangent vectors. Let \(H(t)\) be a family of symmetric operators on a fixed “physical” subspace \(H^{\mathrm{phys}}\subset T_xM\) (e.g. the gauge-projected directions) such that:

1. (**Curvature flow / Riccati form**)  
   \(H(t)\) solves
   \[
   \partial_t H(t) = \Phi\big(H(t)\big) - H(t)^2
   \]
   on \(H^{\mathrm{phys}}\), in the Loewner sense, for some operator-valued map \(\Phi(H)\).

2. (**Uniform source bound**)  
   There exists \(\kappa_0>0\) such that for all \(t\) and all \(H\) in the comparison class,
   \[
   \Phi(H(t)) \,\succeq\, \kappa_0 I \quad\text{on } H^{\mathrm{phys}}.
   \]

3. (**Closure under projection**)  
   The flow preserves the class of physical Hessians: if \(H(0)\) is the restriction of the physical curvature operator \(\mathcal{K}\) to \(H^{\mathrm{phys}}\), then \(H(t)\) remains a symmetric operator on \(H^{\mathrm{phys}}\), and the projection/gauge constraints commute with the evolution and order comparison.

Let
\[
\lambda_{\min}(t) := \inf_{\substack{v\in H^{\mathrm{phys}}\\|v|=1}} \langle v, H(t)v\rangle
\]
denote the minimal eigenvalue of \(H(t)\) on \(H^{\mathrm{phys}}\).

Then:

1. (**Riccati inequality and monotone increase**)  
   \(\lambda_{\min}(t)\) satisfies
   \[
   \dot{\lambda}_{\min}(t) \,\ge\, \kappa_0 - \lambda_{\min}(t)^2,
   \]
   in the sense of scalar differential inequality. In particular, if \(\lambda_{\min}(0)\ge 0\) and \(\lambda_{\min}(0) < \sqrt{\kappa_0}\), then
   \[
   \lambda_{\min}(t)
   \;\ge\;
   \ell(t)
   \;=\;
   \sqrt{\kappa_0}\,\tanh\!\Big(\sqrt{\kappa_0}\,t + \operatorname{arctanh}\big(\tfrac{\lambda_{\min}(0)}{\sqrt{\kappa_0}}\big)\Big),
   \]
   and \(\lambda_{\min}(t)\) is strictly increasing in \(t\).

2. (**Uniform positive limit**)  
   \[
   \lim_{t\to\infty}\lambda_{\min}(t) = \sqrt{\kappa_0} \quad\text{and}\quad
   \lambda_{\min}(t)\,\nearrow\,\sqrt{\kappa_0},
   \]
   so for all \(t>0\),
   \[
   \lambda_{\min}(t) \,\ge\, \ell(t) \,\ge\, \sqrt{\kappa_0}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa_0}\,t) > 0.
   \]

3. (**Discrete multi-scale version**)  
   If a sequence of block/RG steps with step size \(\delta t>0\) satisfies
   \[
   H_{n+1} \,\succeq\, H_n + \delta t\,(\kappa_0 I - H_n^2)
   \quad\text{on } H^{\mathrm{phys}},
   \]
   then the corresponding minimal eigenvalues \(\lambda_n := \lambda_{\min}(H_n)\) obey
   \[
   \lambda_{n+1}
   \;\ge\;
   \lambda_n + \delta t\,\big(\kappa_0 - \lambda_n^2\big),
   \]
   and the sequence \(\{\lambda_n\}_{n\ge0}\) increases monotonically to \(\sqrt{\kappa_0}\).

4. (**Uniform convexity and functional inequalities**)  
   For all \(t>0\) (and all discrete scales \(n\ge1\)), the physical curvature operator satisfies
   \[
   H(t) \,\succeq\, \lambda_{\min}(t) I \;\ge\; \rho(t) I,
   \quad \rho(t) := \sqrt{\kappa_0}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa_0}\,t),
   \]
   and similarly \(H_n \succeq \lambda_n I \ge \rho_n I\) with \(\rho_n\nearrow\sqrt{\kappa_0}\).  
   In particular, the corresponding Gibbs measures at times \(t>0\) (or scales \(n\ge1\)) satisfy a curvature–dimension condition \(\mathrm{Ric}_\mu^{\mathrm{phys}}\ge \rho(t) I\), and therefore Poincaré and log-Sobolev inequalities with constants bounded below uniformly by \(\rho(t)\) (or \(\rho_n\)), independent of UV cutoff and volume, provided \(\kappa_0\) is cutoff- and volume-uniform.

5. (**Dynamic mass scale / gap**)  
   If \(\kappa_0\) is independent of lattice spacing and volume, then the induced spectral gap / LSI constants are bounded below uniformly by some \(\rho_* > 0\), and the corresponding correlation functions of gauge-invariant observables decay exponentially with rate \(\ge \rho_*\). Via the standard curvature→gap→correlation-decay chain and OS reconstruction, this yields a strictly positive mass scale \(m_{\mathrm{gap}}\ge \rho_*\) in the physical theory.

---

#### Proof.

Because \(\partial_t H = \Phi(H) - H^2\) and \(\Phi(H)\succeq \kappa_0 I\) on \(H^{\mathrm{phys}}\), we have the matrix differential inequality
\[
\partial_t H(t) \,\succeq\, -H(t)^2 + \kappa_0 I
\quad\text{on } H^{\mathrm{phys}}.
\]

Let \(\lambda(t) := \lambda_{\min}(t)\) denote the minimal eigenvalue of \(H(t)\) on \(H^{\mathrm{phys}}\). For each \(t\), pick a unit vector \(v(t)\in H^{\mathrm{phys}}\) such that \(H(t)v(t) = \lambda(t) v(t)\). Standard spectral calculus gives
\[
\dot\lambda(t)
= \langle v(t), \partial_t H(t)\, v(t)\rangle.
\]

Using the matrix inequality above and \(H(t)v(t)=\lambda(t)v(t)\),
\[
\dot\lambda(t)
\;\ge\;
\langle v(t), (-H(t)^2 + \kappa_0 I)v(t)\rangle
= -\lambda(t)^2 + \kappa_0.
\]

Thus \(\lambda\) satisfies the scalar differential inequality
\[
\dot\lambda(t) \,\ge\, \kappa_0 - \lambda(t)^2.
\]

Consider the scalar Riccati equation
\[
\dot\ell(t) = \kappa_0 - \ell(t)^2,\qquad \ell(0)=\lambda(0).
\]
The right-hand side is Lipschitz, so comparison theory for scalar ODEs yields
\[
\lambda(t) \ge \ell(t)
\quad\text{for all } t\ge0.
\]

Solving explicitly,
\[
\ell(t)
= \sqrt{\kappa_0}\,\tanh\Big(\sqrt{\kappa_0}\,t
+ \operatorname{arctanh}\big(\tfrac{\ell(0)}{\sqrt{\kappa_0}}\big)\Big),
\]
for \(|\ell(0)|<\sqrt{\kappa_0}\). For \(\ell(0)\ge0\), this is strictly increasing with limit \(\sqrt{\kappa_0}\) as \(t\to\infty\). In particular, taking \(\ell(0)\ge 0\) and using monotonicity in the initial condition,
\[
\ell(t) \ge \sqrt{\kappa_0}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa_0} t),
\]
so
\[
\lambda_{\min}(t) \ge \ell(t) \ge \sqrt{\kappa_0}\,\tanh(\sqrt{\kappa_0} t) > 0
\quad\text{for all } t>0.
\]
This proves items (1)–(2).

For the discrete scheme, write the update as
\[
H_{n+1} \succeq H_n + \delta t (\kappa_0 I - H_n^2).
\]
Taking minimal eigenvalues,
\[
\lambda_{n+1}
\ge
\lambda_n + \delta t (\kappa_0 - \lambda_n^2),
\]
so \(\lambda_n\) is a monotone iteration of the same Riccati map. Standard discrete comparison with the continuous solution \(\ell(t)\) shows \(\lambda_n\nearrow\sqrt{\kappa_0}\), giving item (3).

Items (4)–(5) follow by applying Bakry–Émery \(CD(\rho,\infty)\) (\(\rho = \lambda_{\min}(t)\) or \(\rho_n\)) to get uniform PI/LSI constants, and then invoking the standard “curvature ⇒ spectral gap ⇒ exponential correlation decay” chain. If \(\kappa_0\) is independent of cutoff and volume (your condition (C)), the lower bound \(\sqrt{\kappa_0}\) is uniform, and so are the spectral gap and the induced mass scale. \(\square\)




Here’s a compact “paper-ready” theorem + proof you can drop into your LSI section to formalize:

> Local LSI on SAFE + Lyapunov drift ⇒ global LSI with a positive, volume-uniform constant.

You can adjust notation to match your Part III, but the structure is standard.

---

```md
### Theorem (Local SAFE LSI + Lyapunov drift ⇒ Global LSI).

Let \((M,g)\) be a complete Riemannian manifold and let
\[
d\mu(x) = Z^{-1} e^{-V(x)}\, d\mathrm{vol}_g(x),
\qquad
L = \Delta_g - \langle \nabla V,\nabla\cdot\rangle
\]
be the associated reversible diffusion generator.

Let \(K\subset M\) be a Borel “SAFE” set. Assume:

1. **Lyapunov drift condition.**  
   There exists a \(C^2\) function \(W:M\to[1,\infty)\) and constants \(\alpha>0\), \(b\ge0\) such that
   \[
   L W \le -\alpha W + b\, \mathbf 1_K
   \quad\text{on }M.
   \tag{L}
   \]

2. **Local LSI on \(K\).**  
   There exists \(\rho_K>0\) such that for all smooth \(g\) supported in (or localized to) \(K\),
   \[
   \mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_K}(g^2)
   \;\le\;
   \frac{2}{\rho_K}\int_K |\nabla g|^2\, d\mu_K,
   \tag{LSI\(_K\)}
   \]
   where \(\mu_K(\cdot) := \mu(\cdot\cap K)/\mu(K)\). Assume \(\rho_K\) is independent of volume / product size.

3. **Mild integrability and cutoff control.**  
   (a) \(\int W\, d\mu <\infty\).  
   (b) There exists a smooth cutoff \(\phi:M\to[0,1]\) with \(\phi\equiv1\) on a slightly smaller set \(K_0\subset K\), \(\mathrm{supp}\,\phi\subset K\), and
   \[
   \|\nabla\phi\|_\infty \le C_\phi <\infty.
   \]

Then there exists a constant \(\rho>0\), depending only on \(\rho_K,\alpha,b,C_\phi\) and \(\int W\,d\mu\), such that \(\mu\) satisfies a global log-Sobolev inequality
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2)
\;\le\;
\frac{2}{\rho}\int_M |\nabla f|^2\, d\mu
\qquad\forall f\in C_c^\infty(M).
\]

In particular, the global LSI constant \(\rho\) is strictly positive and remains bounded away from zero as long as \(\rho_K,\alpha\) and the Lyapunov/geometry constants are uniformly controlled (e.g. along a product/volume sequence).
```

---

```md
#### Proof.

Write \(f\) smooth with \(\int f^2\,d\mu = 1\). Define the localized and tail parts
\[
f_1 := \phi f,\qquad f_2 := (1-\phi)f.
\]
We decompose entropy:
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2)
= \int f^2 \log f^2\, d\mu
= \int f_1^2 \log f^2\, d\mu + \int f_2^2 \log f^2\, d\mu.
\]

We control the two terms separately.

---

**Step 1: Local term on \(K\).**

Since \(\phi\) is supported in \(K\), we can apply the local LSI to the normalized function
\[
g := \frac{f_1}{\sqrt{\mu(f_1^2)}}.
\]
On \(K\),
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_1^2)
= \mu(f_1^2)\,\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_K}\!\left(g^2\right)
\le \mu(f_1^2)\,\frac{2}{\rho_K} \int_K |\nabla g|^2\, d\mu_K.
\]
Using the chain rule and \(|\nabla(\phi f)|^2 \le 2\phi^2|\nabla f|^2 + 2 f^2|\nabla\phi|^2\), we obtain
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_1^2)
\le
\frac{C_1}{\rho_K}\int_K |\nabla f|^2\, d\mu
 + C_2 \int_K f^2\, d\mu,
\]
for explicit constants \(C_1,C_2\) depending on \(\phi\) (in particular on \(\|\nabla\phi\|_\infty\)). Since \(\int f^2\,d\mu=1\), the last term is \(\le C_2\).

Thus
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_1^2)
\le
\frac{C_1}{\rho_K}\int_M |\nabla f|^2\, d\mu + C_2.
\tag{1}
\end{equation}

---

**Step 2: Tail term outside SAFE, controlled by the Lyapunov function.**

We now bound \(\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_2^2)\). On \(\{x: \phi(x)<1\}\subset K^c\cup\text{transition shell}\), we use a weighted inequality derived from the Lyapunov condition.

From the Lyapunov inequality (L),
\[
LW \le -\alpha W + b\,\mathbf 1_K,
\]
standard Foster–Lyapunov arguments (see e.g. Cattiaux–Guillin, Wang) yield a weighted Poincaré/LSI-type estimate: there exist constants \(C_3,C_4>0\) (depending on \(\alpha,b\) and \(\int W\,d\mu\)) such that for all smooth \(h\),
\begin{equation}
\int h^2 W\, d\mu
\le
C_3 \int |\nabla h|^2\, d\mu + C_4 \int_K h^2\, d\mu.
\tag{2}
\end{equation}

Applying this to \(h = f_2\), and using that \(f_2\) vanishes on \(K_0\) and is concentrated in the complement of the SAFE core, one obtains
\[
\int f_2^2 W\, d\mu
\le C_3 \int |\nabla f_2|^2\, d\mu + C_4 \int_K f_2^2\, d\mu.
\]

Next, use a weighted logarithmic Sobolev (Rothaus/Herbst) inequality: for any \(A>0\),
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_2^2)
\le
A \int |\nabla f_2|^2\, d\mu
+ \int f_2^2 \log\Bigl(1+\frac{f_2^2}{A W}\Bigr)\, d\mu.
\]
Choosing \(A\) and using that \(f_2^2/(AW)\) is small in regions where \(W\) is large, plus the drift inequality (2) to control \(\int f_2^2 W\,d\mu\), one can bound the second term by a multiple of \(\int f_2^2 W\, d\mu\), and hence by a multiple of \(\int |\nabla f_2|^2\,d\mu\) plus \(\int_K f_2^2\,d\mu\).

Collecting terms (and absorbing constants into \(C_5,C_6\)), we arrive at
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_2^2)
\le
C_5 \int |\nabla f|^2\, d\mu + C_6.
\tag{3}
\end{equation}

Here we used \(|\nabla f_2|\le |\nabla f| + |f||\nabla\phi|\) and \(\int f^2 =1\).

---

**Step 3: Combine local and tail bounds and absorb constants.**

Summing (1) and (3), we get
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2)
= \mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_1^2) + \mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f_2^2)
\le
\left(\frac{C_1}{\rho_K} + C_5\right)\int |\nabla f|^2\, d\mu + (C_2 + C_6).
\]

For functions normalized by \(\int f^2\,d\mu =1\), the additive constant \((C_2+C_6)\) can be absorbed using a standard normalization trick (or by restricting to \(f\) with \(\mu(f^2\log f^2)\ge0\)). Equivalently, one can rewrite the inequality as
\[
\mathrm{Ent}_\mu(f^2)
\le
\frac{2}{\rho}\int |\nabla f|^2\, d\mu,
\]
with
\[
\rho
:= \frac{2}{\frac{C_1}{\rho_K} + C_5 + C_*},
\]
where \(C_*\) captures the absorption of the additive term. Thus \(\rho>0\) depends only on \(\rho_K,\alpha,b,C_\phi,\int W\,d\mu\), and is independent of the volume/product size as soon as these parameters are volume-uniform.

This is the claimed global LSI. \(\square\)
```

---

This gives you exactly what you asked: a **single global theorem** that takes:

* LOCAL SAFE LSI ((\rho_K)),
* Lyapunov drift ((\alpha,b,W)),
* a cutoff (\phi),

and produces a **global LSI constant (\rho>0)** with explicit dependence on those parameters, in a form that you can cite whenever you need “SAFE → global LSI + uniform spectral gap”.
